\sigma=r_{3}\in B=2s+B^{\prime}
we then conclude the existence of a
\bar{\gamma}\left(\beta_{1},n,Q\right)>0
such that

\int_{B_{\sigma}\cap D}|Dh|^{2}\leq\int_{B_{\sigma}\cap K}|Df|^{2}+C\left(E+\mathbf{A}^{2}\right)^{1+\bar{\gamma}}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
9.2
.

Here we proceed as in the proof of
[
11
, Theorem 7.1]
. Choose
\beta_{1}=\frac{1}{8}
and consider the set
B\subset[9/8,2]
given in Proposition
9.4
. Using the coarea formula and the isoperimetric inequality (the argument and the map
\varphi
are the same in the proof of Theorem
7.3
and that of Proposition
8.3
), we find
s\in B
and an integer rectifiable current
R
such that

\partial R=\left\langle T-\mathbf{G}_{f},\varphi,s\right\rangle\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbf{M}(R)\leq CE^{\frac{3}{2}}.

Since
h|_{\partial(D\cap B_{s})}=f|_{\partial(D\cap B_{s})}
we can use
h
in place of
f
in the estimates and, arguing as before (see e.g. the proof of Proposition
8.3
), we get, for a suitable
\gamma>0

\displaystyle\|T\|\left(\mathbf{C}_{s}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Q\left|B_{s}\cap D\right|+\int_{B_{s}\cap D}\frac{|Dg|^{2}}{2}+C(E+\mathbf{A}^{2})^{1+\bar{\gamma}}

(9.40)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:regconvDirichletEnergyEstimate}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle Q\left|B_{s}\cap D\right|+\int_{B_{s}\cap K}\frac{|Df|^{2}}{2}+C\left(E+\mathbf{A}^{2}\right)^{1+\bar{\gamma}}.

On the other hand, by Taylor’s expansion in
[
11
, Remark 5.4]
,

\displaystyle\|T\|\left(\mathbf{C}_{s}\right)

\displaystyle=\|T\|\left(\left(B_{s}\cap D\setminus K\right)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)+\left\|\mathbf{G}_{f}\right\|\left(\left(B_{s}\cap K\right)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\|T\|\left(\left(B_{s}\cap D\setminus K\right)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)+Q\left|K\cap B_{s}\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{K\cap B_{s}}\frac{|Df|^{2}}{2}-C(E+\mathbf{A}^{2})^{1+\bar{\gamma}}.

(9.41)

Hence, from (
9.40
) and (
9.1
), we get
\mathbf{e}_{T}\left(B_{s}\cap D\setminus K\right)\leq C\left(E+\mathbf{A}^{2}\right)^{1+\bar{\gamma}}
.
This is enough to conclude the proof. Indeed, let
A\subset B_{9/8}\cap D
be a Borel set. Using the higher integrability of
|Df|
in
K
(see (
9.8
)
)
and possibly selecting a smaller
\bar{\gamma}>0,
we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{T}(A)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{e}_{T}(A\cap K)+\mathbf{e}_{T}(A\setminus K)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{A\cap K}\frac{|Df|^{2}}{2}+C\left(E+\mathbf{A}^{2}\right)^{1+\bar{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq C|A\cap K|^{\frac{p_{1}-1}{p_{1}}}\left(\int_{A\cap K}|Df|^{q_{1}}\right)^{2/q_{1}}+C\left(E+\mathbf{A}^{2}\right)^{1+\bar{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq C|A|^{\frac{p_{1}-1}{p_{1}}}\left(E+\mathbf{A}^{2}\right)+C\left(E+\mathbf{A}^{2}\right)^{1+\bar{\gamma}}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Here we proceed exactly as in the proof of
[
11
, Theorem 2.4]
. Assume
r=1
and
x=0.
Choose
\beta_{11}<\min\left\{\frac{1}{4},\frac{\gamma_{11}}{2\left(1+\gamma_{11}\right)}\right\},
where
\gamma_{11}
is the constant in Theorem
9.4
.
Let
f
be the
E^{\beta_{11}}
-Lipschitz approximation of
T
. Clearly (
9.1
) and (
9.2
) follow directly from Proposition
6.4
, if
\gamma<\beta_{11}.
Set next
A:=\left\{\mathbf{me}_{T}>2^{-m}(E+\mathbf{A}^{2})^{2\beta_{11}}\right\}\cap B_{9/8}.
By Proposition
6.4
we have
|A|\leq C(E+\mathbf{A}^{2})^{1-2\beta_{11}}
. If
\varepsilon_{A}>0
is sufficiently small, apply (
6.9
) and the estimate (
9.5
) to
A
in order to conclude

\left|B_{1}\cap D\setminus K\right|\leq C(E+\mathbf{A}^{2})^{-2\beta_{11}}\mathbf{e}_{T}(A)\leq C(E+\mathbf{A}^{2})^{\gamma_{11}-2\beta_{11}\left(1+\gamma_{11}\right)}\left(E+\mathbf{A}^{2}\right).

By our choice of
\gamma_{11}
and
\beta_{11},
this last inequality gives (
9.3
) for some positive
\gamma_{1}
. Finally, set
S=\mathbf{G}_{f}.
Recalling the strong Almgren estimate (
9.5
) and the Taylor expansion in
[
11
, Remark 5.4]
we conclude for every
0<\sigma\leq 1

\displaystyle\left|\|T\|\left(\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}\right)-Q|D|-\int_{B_{\sigma}\cap D}\frac{|Df|^{2}}{2}\right|

(9.42)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{e}_{T}\left(B_{\sigma}\cap D\setminus K\right)+\mathbf{e}_{S}\left(B_{\sigma}\cap D\setminus K\right)+\left|\mathbf{e}_{S}\left(B_{\sigma}\cap D\right)-\int_{B_{\sigma}\cap D}\frac{|Df|^{2}}{2}\right|

(9.43)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(E+\mathbf{A}^{2}\right)^{1+\gamma_{11}}+C\left|B_{\sigma}\cap D\setminus K\right|+C\operatorname{Lip}(f)^{2}\int_{B_{\sigma}\cap D}|Df|^{2}

(9.44)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(E+\mathbf{A}^{2}\right)^{1+\gamma_{11}}.

(9.45)

The
L^{\infty}
bound follows from Proposition
6.4
and we finish the proof.
∎

10.
Center manifold and normal approximation

This section is devoted to prove an analog of
[
8
, Theorem 8.13]
, namely to construct, in a neighborhood of a flat point
p
, a smooth
C^{3,\alpha}
submanifold with boundary
\Gamma
and a normal multivalued map
N
on it. The first is, roughly, an approximation of the average of the sheets lying over the unique tangent plane
V
to
T
at
p
. The second is a more accurate approximation of the current
T
, which compared to the one in Section
6
has the additional property of having (almost) zero average.

We start by introducing the spherical excess and the cylindrical excess with respect to a general plane.

Definition 10.1
.

Given a current
T
as in Assumption
4.5
and
2
-dimensional planes
V,V^{\prime}
, we define the
excess of
T
in balls
and cylinders with respect to planes
V,V^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{r}(x),V)

\displaystyle:=\left(2\pi\,r^{2}\right)^{-1}\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{r}(x)}|\vec{T}-\vec{V}|^{2}\,d\|T\|,

\displaystyle\qquad{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{r}(x,V),V^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\left(2\pi\,r^{2}\right)^{-1}\int_{{\mathbf{C}}_{r}(x,V)}|\vec{T}-\vec{V}^{\prime}|^{2}\,d\|T\|\,.

Definition 10.2

(Optimal planes)
.

For the case of balls we define the spherical excess as follows. The
optimal spherical excess
at some
x\in{\rm spt}(T)\setminus\Gamma
is given by

{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{r}(x)):=\min_{V}{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{r}(x),V),

(10.1)

but in the case of
x\in\Gamma
we define the
optimal boundary spherical excess
as

{\mathbf{E}}^{\flat}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{r}(x)):=\min\{{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{r}(x),V):V\supset T_{x}\Gamma\}.

The plane
V
which minimizes
{\mathbf{E}}
, resp.
{\mathbf{E}}^{\flat}
, is not unique but since for notational purposes it is convenient to define
a unique “height”
\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{r}(x))
we set

\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{r}(x)):=\min\big\{\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{r}(x),V):\mbox{ $V$ optimizes ${\mathbf{E}}$ (resp. ${\mathbf{E}}^{\flat})$}\big\}\,.

(10.2)

In the case of cylinders we denote by
{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{r}(x,V))={\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{r}(x,V),V)
and
\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{r}(x,V))=\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{r}(x,V),V)
.

We recall that under the above assumptions
{\mathbf{C}}_{5R_{0}}={\mathbf{C}}_{5R_{0}}(0,V_{0})
and
{\mathbf{p}}_{\sharp}T\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{5R_{0}}=Q\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket
, where
D\subset B_{5R_{0}}
is one of the two connected components in which
B_{5R_{0}}
is subdivided by the curve
\gamma={\mathbf{p}}(\Gamma)
. Moreover
T_{0}\Gamma=\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}
and in particular
\Gamma\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{5R_{0}}=\{(t,\psi(t))\}=\{(t,\psi_{1}(t),\bar{\psi}(t))\}
, where
\psi_{1}:(-5R_{0},5R_{0})\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
\bar{\psi}:(-5R_{0},5R_{0})\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. In particular
\gamma
is the graph of
\psi_{1}
and without loss of generality we assume that
D=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in B_{5R_{0}}:x_{2}>\psi_{1}(x_{1})\}
, namely it is the upper half of
B_{5R_{0}}\setminus\gamma
.

In this section we will then work under the following assumptions.

Assumption 10.3
.

p=q=(0,0)
,
V=V_{0}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\}
,
Q
,
T
, and
\Gamma
are as in Assumption
6.2
in the cylinder
{\mathbf{C}}_{5R_{0}}
, where
R_{0}\geq 1+\sqrt{2}
is a sufficiently large geometric constant which will be specified later. Moreover
Q\left\llbracket{V_{0}}\right\rrbracket
is the (unique) tangent cone to
T
at
0
.

We moreover assume in the sequel that

{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{5R_{0}}(0,V_{0}))+\mathbf{A}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{CM},

(10.3)

for some small positive parameter
\varepsilon_{CM}=\varepsilon_{CM}(n,Q,R_{0})
.

Under the above assumptions we show now that the height of
T
in
{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}}
is also under control.

Lemma 10.4
.

There are constants
\varepsilon_{CM},C
depending on
Q,n
and
R_{0}
such that, if Assumption
10.3
holds, then for all
p\in\Gamma
and
r>0
such that
{\mathbf{C}}_{5r}(p,V_{0})\subset{\mathbf{C}}_{5R_{0}}
, we have

\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{4r}(p,V_{0}))\leq Cr({\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{5r}(p,V_{0}))+r\mathbf{A})^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.

(10.4)

Proof.

We divide the proof into two steps.

Step 1:

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in{\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{4r}(p,V_{0})}|{\mathbf{p}}^{\perp}_{V_{0}}(z-p)|^{2}\leq Cr^{-2}\int_{{\mathbf{C}}_{9r/2}(p,V_{0})}|{\mathbf{p}}^{\perp}_{V_{0}}(z-p)|^{2}d\|T\|(z)+C_{0}\mathbf{A}^{2}r^{4}
.

This is shown in
[
8
, Lemma 6.6]
and carries over word by word to our setting as the only part where the stationarity of the associated integral varifold is needed, is for the harmonicity of the coordinate functions. This however is true, as we test with functions which are supported away from the boundary of
T
. We use this to apply a Moser iteration scheme and estimate the
L^{\infty}
norm by the limsup of the
L^{p}
norms as
p\to\infty
.

Step 2:

\displaystyle r^{-2}\int_{{\mathbf{C}}_{9r/2}(p,V_{0})}|{\mathbf{p}}^{\perp}_{V_{0}}(z-p)|^{2}d\|T\|(z)\leq C\ {\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{5r}(p,V_{0}))r^{2}+C\mathbf{A}r^{3}
.

Also for this, the proof of
[
8
, Lemma 6.7]
carries over as the difference to our situation is a factor
Q
in the monotonicity formula (Theorem
3.2
). From there, we estimate the remainder term by
r^{2}({\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{5r}(0,V_{0}))+\mathbf{A})
.
∎

10.1.
Whitney decomposition

We specify next some notation which will be recurrent when dealing with squares inside
V_{0}
.
For each
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\mathscr{C}}_{j}
denotes the family of closed squares
L
of
V_{0}
of the form

[a_{1},a_{1}+2\ell]\times[a_{2},a_{2}+2\ell]\times\{0\}\subset V_{0}\,

(10.5)

which intersect
D
,
where
2\,\ell=2^{1-j}=:2\,\ell(L)
is the side-length of the square,

a_{i}\in 2^{1-j}{\mathbb{Z}}

\forall i
and we require in
addition
-4\leq a_{i}\leq a_{i}+2\ell\leq 4
.
To avoid cumbersome notation, we will usually drop the factor
\{0\}
in (
10.5
) and treat each squares, its subsets and its points as subsets and elements of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Thus, for the
center
x_{L}
of
L
we will use the notation
x_{L}=(a_{1}+\ell,a_{2}+\ell)
, although the precise one is
(a_{1}+\ell,a_{2}+\ell,0,\ldots,0)
.
Next we set
{\mathscr{C}}:=\bigcup_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathscr{C}}_{j}
.
If
H
and
L
are two squares in
{\mathscr{C}}
with
H\subset L
, then we call
L
an
ancestor
of
H
and
H
a
descendant
of
L
. When in addition
\ell(L)=2\ell(H)
,
H
is
a child
of
L
and
L

the parent
of
H
. Moreover, if
H\cap L\neq\emptyset
but they are not contained in each other, we call them
neighbours
.

Definition 10.5
.

A
Whitney decomposition of
\overline{D}\cap[-4,4]^{2}\subset V_{0}
consists of a closed set
{\bm{\Delta}}\subset[-4,4]^{2}\cap\overline{D}
and a family
\mathscr{W}\subset{\mathscr{C}}
satisfying the following properties:

(w1)

{\bm{\Delta}}\cup\bigcup_{L\in\mathscr{W}}L\cap\overline{D}=[-4,4]^{2}\cap\overline{D}
and
{\bm{\Delta}}
does not intersect any element of
\mathscr{W}
;

(w2)

the interiors of any pair of distinct squares
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathscr{W}
are disjoint;

(w3)

if
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathscr{W}
have nonempty intersection, then
\frac{1}{2}\ell(L_{1})\leq\ell(L_{2})\leq 2\,\ell(L_{1})
.

Remark 10.6
.

Because of (w1) we will assume that any
L\in{\mathscr{W}}
intersects
\overline{D}
.

Observe that (w1) - (w3) imply

{\rm sep}\,({\bm{\Delta}},L):=\inf\{|x-y|:x\in L,y\in{\bm{\Delta}}\}\geq 2\ell(L)\quad\mbox{for every $L\in\mathscr{W}$,}

(10.6)

since there is an infinite chain of neighbouring squares
\{L_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}
with
L_{0}=L
,
{\rm{dist}}({\bm{\Delta}},L_{i})\to 0
and
\ell(L_{i})\geq 2\ell(L_{i+1})
for all
i
.
However, we do
not
require any inequality of the form

{\rm sep}\,({\bm{\Delta}},L)\leq C\ell(L)
, although this would be customary for what is commonly
called a Whitney decomposition in the literature.

Assumption 10.7
.

In the rest of this section we will use several different parameters:

(a)

\delta_{1}
and
\beta_{1}
are two small geometric constants which depends only on
Q
,
n
, the constant
\gamma_{1}
of Theorem
9.1
, in fact they will be chosen smaller than
\frac{\gamma_{1}}{8}
and
\delta_{1}\leq\frac{\beta_{1}}{2}
;

(b)

M_{0}
is a large geometric constant which depends only on
\delta_{1}
, while
N_{0}\geq\frac{\ln(132\sqrt{2})}{\ln(2)}
is a large natural number which will be chosen depending on
\beta_{1},\delta_{1}
, and
M_{0}
;

(c)

C^{\flat}_{e}
is a large constant
C^{\flat}_{e}(\beta_{1},\delta_{1},M_{0},N_{0})
, while
C^{\natural}_{e}
is larger and depends also on
C^{\flat}_{e}
;

(d)

C_{h}
is large and depends on
\beta_{1},\delta_{1},M_{0},N_{0},C^{\flat}_{e}
and
C^{\natural}_{e}
;

(e)

the small threshold
\varepsilon_{CM}
is the last to be chosen, it depends on all the previous parameters and also on the constant
\varepsilon_{A}
of Theorem
9.1
.

Definition 10.8
.

For each square
L\in\mathscr{C}
we set
r_{L}:=\sqrt{2}M_{0}\ell(L)
and we say that
L
is an
interior square
if
{\rm{dist}}(x_{L},\gamma)\geq 64r_{L}
, otherwise we say that
L
is a
boundary square
and we use, respectively, the notation
\mathscr{C}^{\natural}
for the interior squares contained in
D
and
\mathscr{C}^{\flat}
for the boundary squares. Next, we define a corresponding
(n+2)
-dimensional balls
{\mathbf{B}}_{L}
, resp.
{\mathbf{B}}^{\flat}_{L}
, for such
L
’s:

(a)

If
L\in\mathscr{C}^{\natural}
, we pick a point
p_{L}=(x_{L},y_{L})\in{\rm spt}(T)\cap(\{x_{L}\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
and we set
{\mathbf{B}}_{L}:={\mathbf{B}}_{64r_{L}}(p_{L})
;

(b)

If
L\in\mathscr{C}^{\flat}
, we pick
x^{\flat}_{L}=(t,\psi_{1}(t))\in\gamma
such that
{\rm{dist}}(x_{L},\gamma)=|x^{\flat}_{L}-x_{L}|
, define
p^{\flat}_{L}=(t,\psi(t))\in\Gamma\cap(\{x_{L}^{\flat}\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
and set
{\mathbf{B}}^{\flat}_{L}={\mathbf{B}}_{2^{7}64r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L})
.

We are now ready to prescribe
N_{0}
: we require the inequality

2^{7}64r_{L}\leq 2^{7}64\sqrt{2}M_{0}2^{-{N_{0}}}\leq 1\,,

(10.7)

so that, in particular, all the balls
{\mathbf{B}}_{L}
and
{\mathbf{B}}^{\flat}_{L}
considered above are contained in the cylinder
{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}}
.

The following remark will be useful in the sequel.

Remark 10.9
.

If
L\in\mathscr{C}^{\flat}
and
J
is the parent of
L
, then
J\in\mathscr{C}^{\flat}
, while if
L\in\mathscr{C}^{\natural}
, then every child of
L
is an element of
\mathscr{C}^{\natural}
. In fact, if
H
and
L
are two squares with nonempty intersection,
\ell(H)<\ell(L)
and
H
is a boundary cube, then necessarily
L
is a boundary cube too.

Remark 10.10
.

Fix
L\in\mathscr{C}^{\flat}
and subdivide it into the canonical four squares
M
with half the sidelength. For
M
any of the following three cases can occur:
M
might be a boundary square, an interior square, or might simply not belong to
\mathscr{C}^{\flat}\cup\mathscr{C}^{\natural}
(i.e.
M\cap\overline{D}=\emptyset
). However, because of the enlarged radius for boundary squares, it still holds that the ball of a child is contained in the ball of its parent (compare to Proposition
11.1
(i)
). Moreover,
{\mathbf{B}}_{L}^{\flat}\supset L
for any boundary square
L
.

We are now ready to define the refining procedure leading to the desired Whitney decomposition.

Definition 10.11
.

First of all we set
{\bm{m}}_{0}:={\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{5R_{0}})+\|\psi\|^{2}_{C^{3,\alpha}(]-5R_{0},5R_{0}[)}
.
We start with all
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}\cup{\mathscr{C}}^{\sharp}
with
\ell(L)=2^{-N_{0}}
and we assign all of them to
{\mathscr{S}}
. Next, inductively, for each
j>N_{0}
and each
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}_{j}\cup{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}_{j}
such that its parent belongs to
{\mathscr{S}}
we assign to
{\mathscr{S}}
or to
{\mathscr{W}}={\mathscr{W}}^{e}\cup{\mathscr{W}}^{h}\cup{\mathscr{W}}^{n}
in the following way:

(EX)

L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
if
{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L})>C^{\natural}_{e}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(L)^{2-2\delta_{1}}
, resp. if
{\mathbf{E}}^{\flat}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\flat}_{L})>C^{\flat}_{e}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(L)^{2-2\delta_{1}}
;

(HT)

L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{h}
if
L\not\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
and

\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L})\geq C_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}}
, resp.
\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\flat}_{L})\geq C_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}}
;

(NN)

L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{n}
if
L\not\in{\mathscr{W}}^{h}\cup{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
but there is a
J\in{\mathscr{W}}
such that
\ell(J)=2\ell(L)
and
L\cap J\neq\emptyset
;

(S)

L\in{\mathscr{S}}
if none of three conditions above are satisfied.

We denote by
{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}_{j}:={\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}\cap{\mathscr{C}}_{j}
,
{\mathscr{C}}^{\sharp}_{j}:={\mathscr{C}}^{\sharp}\cap{\mathscr{C}}_{j}
,
{\mathscr{S}}_{j}:={\mathscr{S}}\cap{\mathscr{C}}_{j}
,
{\mathscr{W}}_{j}:={\mathscr{W}}\cap{\mathscr{C}}_{j}
,
{\mathscr{W}}^{e}_{j}:={\mathscr{W}}^{e}\cap{\mathscr{C}}_{j}
,
{\mathscr{W}}^{h}_{j}:={\mathscr{W}}^{h}\cap{\mathscr{C}}_{j}
and
{\mathscr{W}}^{n}_{j}:={\mathscr{W}}^{n}\cap{\mathscr{C}}_{j}
.
Finally, we set

{\bm{\Delta}}:=([-4,4]^{2}\cap\overline{D})\setminus\bigcup_{L\in{\mathscr{W}}}L=\bigcap_{j\geq N_{0}}\bigcup_{L\in{\mathscr{S}}_{j}}L\,.

(10.8)

A simple consequence of our refining procedure is the following proposition which we will prove in the next section.

Proposition 10.12
.

Let
V_{0},Q,T
, and
\Gamma
be as in Assumption
10.3
and assume the parameter
N_{0}
satisfies (
10.7
). Then
({\bm{\Delta}},{\mathscr{W}})
is a Whitney decomposition of
\overline{D}\cap[-4,4]^{2}
. Moreover, for any choice of
M_{0}
and
N_{0}
, there is
C^{\star}(M_{0},N_{0})
such that, if
C^{\flat}_{e}
, and

C^{\natural}_{e}/C^{\flat}_{e}
,
C_{h}/C^{\natural}_{e}
,
are larger than
C^{\star}
, then

(a)

{\mathscr{W}}_{N_{0}}=\emptyset
;

(b)

if
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}\cap{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
then the parent of
L
belongs to
{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
.

Moreover, the following estimates hold for some geometric constant
C
depending on
\beta_{1}
and
\delta_{1}
, provided
\varepsilon_{CM}
is sufficiently small (depending on all the previous parameters as detailed in Assumption
10.7
):

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}^{\flat}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\flat}_{L})\leq CC^{\flat}_{e}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(L)^{2-2\delta_{1}},\;

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\;\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\flat}_{L})\leq CC_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}},\;\forall L\in{\mathscr{W}}\cap{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}\,,

(10.9)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L})\leq CC^{\natural}_{e}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(L)^{2-2\delta_{1}}\;

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\;\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L})\leq CC_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}},\;\forall L\in{\mathscr{W}}\cap{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}\,.

(10.10)

10.2.
Construction of the center manifold

First of all for each
{\mathbf{B}}_{L}
and
{\mathbf{B}}^{\flat}_{L}
, we let
V_{L}
be the choice of optimal plane for the excess and the height in the sense of Definition
10.2
: note that for boundary squares, namely in
{\mathbf{B}}^{\flat}_{L}
, the plane
V_{L}
optimizes the excess
{\mathbf{E}}^{\flat}
, and thus it is constrained to contain the line
T_{p^{\flat}_{L}}\Gamma
. The following key lemma allows us to apply Theorem
9.1
(and its interior version
[
11
, Theorem 2.4]
) to corresponding cylinders.

Lemma 10.13
.

For any choice of the other parameters, if
\varepsilon_{CM}
is sufficiently small, the following holds for every
L\in{\mathscr{S}}\cup{\mathscr{W}}
.

(a)

If
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
, then
T
satisfies the assumptions of
[
11
, Theorem 2.4]
in
{\mathbf{C}}_{32r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{L})
.

(b)

If
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
, then
T
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
9.1
in
{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}32r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{L})
.

The corresponding
Q
-valued strong Lipschitz approximations will be denoted by
f_{L}
and will be called
V_{L}
-approximations.

Given a square
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
which belongs to
{\mathscr{S}}\cup{\mathscr{W}}
, we denote by
D_{L}\subset B_{2^{7}24r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V^{\flat}_{L})
the domain of the function
f_{L}
, which coincides with the orthogonal projection on
p^{\flat}_{L}+V^{\flat}_{L}
of
{\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}24r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V^{\flat}_{L})
. Note in particular that
\partial D_{L}\cap B_{2^{7}24r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V^{\flat}_{L})
is the projection of
\Gamma\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}24r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V^{\flat}_{L})
onto
p^{\flat}_{L}+V^{\flat}_{L}
, which we will denote by
\gamma_{L}
. Likewise, we denote by
g_{L}
the function over
\gamma_{L}
whose graph gives
\Gamma\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}24r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V^{\flat}_{L})
. In particular, Theorem
9.1
implies that
f_{L}|_{\gamma_{L}}=Q\left\llbracket{g_{L}}\right\rrbracket
.
We now regularize the averages
{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{L}
to suitable harmonic functions
h_{L}
in the following fashion.

Definition 10.14
.

We denote by
h_{L}
the harmonic function on
B_{16r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{L})
, resp.
D_{L}\cap B_{2^{7}16r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{L})
, for
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
, resp.
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
, such that the boundary value of
h_{L}
on the respective domain is given by
{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{L}
(in particular it coincides with
g_{L}
on
\gamma_{L}
).
h_{L}
will be called
tilted harmonic interpolating function
.

In order to complete the description of our algorithm we need a second important technical lemma.

Lemma 10.15
.

Consider
L\in{\mathscr{S}}\cup{\mathscr{W}}
. For every
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
, resp.
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
, there is a smooth function
u_{L}:D\cap B_{2^{7}8r_{L}}({\mathbf{p}}_{0}(p^{\flat}_{L}),V_{0})\to V_{0}^{\perp}
, resp.
u_{L}:B_{8r_{L}}({\mathbf{p}}_{0}(p_{L}),V_{0})\to V_{0}^{\perp}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{u_{L}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{8r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{G}_{h_{L}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{8r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{0}),\qquad\mbox{resp.}

(10.11)

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{u_{L}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{8r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{G}_{h_{L}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{8r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{0}).

(10.12)

The function
u_{L}
will be called
interpolating function
.

The center manifold is the result of gluing the interpolating functions appropriately. To that we fix a bump function
\vartheta\in C^{\infty}_{c}((-\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2})^{2})
which is identically
1
on
[-1,1]^{2}
and define

\vartheta_{L}(x):=\vartheta\left(\frac{x-x_{L}}{\ell(L)}\right)\,.

Hence, for any fixed
j\geq N_{0}
we define

{\mathscr{P}}^{j}:={\mathscr{S}}_{j}\cup\bigcup_{i\leq j}{\mathscr{W}}_{i}

(10.13)

and the following function
{\bm{\varphi}}_{j}
, defined over
D\cap[-4,4]^{2}\subset V_{0}
and taking values in
V_{0}^{\perp}

{\bm{\varphi}}_{j}(x):=\frac{\sum_{L\in{\mathscr{P}}^{j}}\vartheta_{L}(x)u_{L}(x)}{\sum_{H\in{\mathscr{P}}^{j}}\vartheta_{H}(x)}\,.

(10.14)

The center manifold is the graph of the function
{\bm{\varphi}}
which is the limit of
{\bm{\varphi}}_{j}
as explained in the statement of the next theorem.

Theorem 10.16

(Center manifold)
.

Let
T
be as in Assumption
10.3
and assume that the parameters satisfy the conditions of Assumption
10.7
. Then there is a positive
\omega
(depending only on
\delta_{1}
and
\beta_{1}
), with the following properties:

(a)

{\bm{\varphi}}_{j}|_{\gamma}=g
for every
j
;

(b)

\|{\bm{\varphi}}_{j}\|_{C^{3,\omega}}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}
for some constant
C
which depends on
\beta_{1},\delta_{1},M_{0},N_{0},C^{\natural}_{e},C^{\flat}_{e}
, and
C_{h}
, but not on
\varepsilon_{CM}
;

(c)

For every
k,k^{\prime}\geq j+2
,
{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}={\bm{\varphi}}_{k^{\prime}}
on every cube
L\in{\mathscr{W}}_{j}
;

(d)

{\bm{\varphi}}_{j}
converges uniformly to a
C^{3,\omega}
function
{\bm{\varphi}}
.

Definition 10.17
.

The graph of the function
{\bm{\varphi}}
will be called
center manifold
and denoted by
{\mathcal{M}}
. We will define
{\bm{\Phi}}(x):=(x,{\bm{\varphi}}(x))
as the graphical parametrization of
{\mathcal{M}}
over
[-4,4]^{2}\cap\bar{D}
. The set
{\bm{\Phi}}({\bm{\Delta}})
will be called the
contact set
, while for every
L\in{\mathscr{W}}
the corresponding
{\mathcal{L}}:={\bm{\Phi}}(L\cap D)
will be called
Whitney region
.

10.3.
The
{\mathcal{M}}
-normal approximation and related estimates

In what follows we assume that the conclusions of Theorem
10.16
apply.
For any Borel set
{\mathcal{V}}\subset{\mathcal{M}}
we will denote
by
|{\mathcal{V}}|
its
{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
-measure and will write
\int_{\mathcal{V}}f
for the integral of
f

with respect to
{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
.

{\mathcal{B}}_{r}(q)
denotes the geodesic balls in
{\mathcal{M}}
. Moreover, we refer to
[
12
]

for all the relevant notation pertaining to the differentiation of (multiple valued)
maps defined on
{\mathcal{M}}
, induced currents, differential geometric tensors and so on.

Assumption 10.18
.

We fix the following notation and assumptions.

(U)

{\mathbf{U}}:=\big\{x+y:x\in{\mathcal{M}},|y|<1,\mbox{\; and \;}y\perp{\mathcal{M}}\big\}
.

(P)

{\mathbf{p}}:{\mathbf{U}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
is the map defined by
(x+y)\mapsto x
.

(R)

For any choice of the other parameters, we assume
{\varepsilon}_{CM}
to be so small that

{\mathbf{p}}
extends to
C^{2,\kappa}(\bar{\mathbf{U}})
and

{\mathbf{p}}^{-1}(y)=y+\overline{B_{1}(0,(T_{y}{\mathcal{M}})^{\perp})}
for every
y\in{\mathcal{M}}
.

(L)

We denote by
\partial_{l}{\mathbf{U}}:={\mathbf{p}}^{-1}(\partial{\mathcal{M}})

the
lateral boundary
of
{\mathbf{U}}
.

The following is then a corollary of Theorem
10.16
and the construction algorithm.

Corollary 10.19
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
10.16
and of Assumption
10.18

we have:

(i)

{\rm spt}(\partial(T\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{U}}))\subset\partial_{l}{\mathbf{U}}
,

{\rm spt}(T\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits[-\frac{7}{2},\frac{7}{2}]^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n})\subset{\mathbf{U}}

and
{\mathbf{p}}_{\sharp}(T\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{U}})=Q\left\llbracket{{\mathcal{M}}}\right\rrbracket
;

(ii)

{\rm spt}(\langle T,{\mathbf{p}},{\bm{\Phi}}(q)\rangle)\subset\big\{y\,:|{\bm{\Phi}}(q)-y|\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}}\big\}
for every
q\in L\in{\mathscr{W}}
, where
C
depends on all the parameters except
{\varepsilon}_{CM}
;

(iii)

\langle T,{\mathbf{p}},p\rangle=Q\left\llbracket{p}\right\rrbracket
for every
p\in{\bm{\Phi}}({\bm{\Delta}})\cup(\Gamma\cap\partial{\mathcal{M}})
.

The main reason for introducing the center manifold of Theorem
10.16
is that we are able to pair it with a good approximating map defined on it.

Definition 10.20

(
{\mathcal{M}}
-normal approximation)
.

An
{\mathcal{M}}
-normal approximation of
T
is given by a pair
({\mathcal{K}},F)
such that

(A1)

F:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}({\mathbf{U}})
is Lipschitz (with respect to the geodesic distance on
{\mathcal{M}}
) and takes the special form

F(x)=\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{x+N_{i}(x)}\right\rrbracket
, with
N_{i}(x)\perp T_{x}{\mathcal{M}}
.

(A2)

{\mathcal{K}}\subset{\mathcal{M}}
is closed and
\mathbf{T}_{F}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{p}}^{-1}({\mathcal{K}})=T\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{p}}^{-1}({\mathcal{K}})
.

(A3)

{\mathcal{K}}
contains
{\bm{\Phi}}\big({\bm{\Delta}}\cap[-\frac{7}{2},\frac{7}{2}]^{2}\big)
and
\Gamma\cap{\bm{\Phi}}(\bar{D}\cap[-\frac{7}{2},\frac{7}{2}]^{2})
, and on the latter two sets the map
N
equals
Q\left\llbracket{0}\right\rrbracket
.

The map
N=\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{N_{i}}\right\rrbracket:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}({\mathbb{R}}^{2+n})
is
the normal part of
F
.

Theorem 10.21

(Existence and local estimates for the
{\mathcal{M}}
-normal approximation)
.

Let
\gamma_{2}:=\frac{\gamma_{1}}{4}
, with
\gamma_{1}
the constant
of Theorem
9.1
.
Under the hypotheses of Theorem
10.16
and Assumption
10.18
,
if
{\varepsilon}_{CM}
is suitably small (depending upon all other parameters but not the current
T
), then
there is an
{\mathcal{M}}
-normal approximation
({\mathcal{K}},F)
such that
the following estimates hold on every Whitney region
{\mathcal{L}}
associated to
a cube
L\in{\mathscr{W}}
, with constants
C=C(\beta_{1},\delta_{1},M_{0},N_{0},C^{\natural}_{e},C^{\flat}_{e},C_{h})>0:

\displaystyle{\rm{Lip}}(N|_{\mathcal{L}})\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\gamma_{2}}\ell(L)^{\gamma_{2}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|N|_{\mathcal{L}}\|_{C^{0}}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}},

(10.15)

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{L}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}|+\|\mathbf{T}_{F}-T\|({\mathbf{p}}^{-1}({\mathcal{L}}))\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1+\gamma_{2}}\ell(L)^{4+\gamma_{2}},

(10.16)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{L}}}|DN|^{2}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}\,\ell(L)^{4-2\delta_{1}}\,.

(10.17)

Moreover, for any
a>0
and any Borel
{\mathcal{V}}\subset{\mathcal{L}}
, we have
(
for
C=C(\beta_{1},\delta_{1},M_{0},N_{0},C^{\flat}_{e},C^{\natural}_{e},C_{h})
)

\int_{\mathcal{V}}|{\bm{\eta}}\circ N|\leq{C{\bm{m}}_{0}\left(\ell(L)^{5+\nicefrac{{\beta_{1}}}{{3}}}+a\,\ell(L)^{2+\nicefrac{{\gamma_{2}}}{{2}}}|{\mathcal{V}}|\right)}+\frac{C}{a}\int_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathcal{G}}\big(N,Q\left\llbracket{{\bm{\eta}}\circ N}\right\rrbracket\big)^{2+\gamma_{2}}\,.

(10.18)

From (
10.15
) - (
10.17
) it is not difficult to infer analogous “global versions” of the estimates.

Corollary 10.22

(Global estimates for the
{\mathcal{M}}
-normal approximation)
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
be
the domain
{\bm{\Phi}}\big(D\cap[-\frac{7}{2},\frac{7}{2}]^{2}\big)
and
N
the map of Theorem
10.21
. Then, (again with
C=C(\beta_{1},\delta_{1},M_{0},N_{0},C^{\natural}_{e},C^{\flat}_{e},C_{h})
)

\displaystyle{\rm{Lip}}(N|_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}})\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\gamma_{2}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|N|_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}}\|_{C^{0}}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}},

(10.19)

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}|+\|\mathbf{T}_{F}-T\|({\mathbf{p}}^{-1}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}))\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1+\gamma_{2}},

(10.20)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}}|DN|^{2}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}\,.

(10.21)

In addition, since
N=Q\left\llbracket{0}\right\rrbracket
on
{\bm{\Gamma}}\cap{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
, we also get

\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}}|N|^{2}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}\,.

(10.22)

10.4.
Additional
L^{1}
estimate

While the estimates claimed so far have all appropriate counterparts in the papers
[
13
]
and
[
8
]
, we will need an additional important estimate which is noticed here for the first time, even though it is still a consequence of the same arguments leading to Theorem
10.16
and Theorem
10.21
.

Proposition 10.23
.

Consider the function
f:B_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the property that
\mathbf{G}_{f}=\mathbf{T}_{F}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{3}
. For every
L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
we then have the estimate

\|{\bm{\varphi}}-{\bm{\eta}}\circ f\|_{L^{1}(L)}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{3/4}\ell(L)^{4}

(10.23)

and in particular, as long as
r\leq 3
is a radius such that
\ell(L)\leq r
for every
L\in{\mathscr{W}}
with
L\cap B_{r}\neq\emptyset
, we have the estimate

\|{\bm{\varphi}}-{\bm{\eta}}\circ f\|_{L^{1}(B_{r})}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{3/4}r^{4}\,.

(10.24)

11.
Tilting of optimal planes

We estimate the changes of excess and height when tilting the reference planes of nearby squares.

Proposition 11.1

(Tilting of optimal planes)
.

Let
Q
,
T
and
\Gamma
be as in Assumption
10.3
and recall the parameters of Assumption
10.7
. There are constants
\overline{C}=\overline{C}(\beta_{1},\delta_{1},M_{0},N_{0},

C_{e}^{\natural},C_{e}^{\flat})>0
and
C=C(\beta_{1},\delta_{1},M_{0},N_{0},C_{e}^{\natural},C_{e}^{\flat},C_{h})>0
such that, if
\varepsilon_{CM}=\varepsilon_{CM}(Q,n,R_{0},C_{h})

>0
is small enough, for any
H,L\in{\mathscr{S}}\cup{\mathscr{W}}
with
H
being equal or a descendant of
L
we have

(i)

{\mathbf{B}}^{\Box}_{H}\subset{\mathbf{B}}^{\Box}_{L}\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{4R_{0}}
,

(ii)

|V_{H}-V_{L}|\leq\overline{C}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\ell(L)^{1-\delta_{1}}
,

(iii)

|V_{H}-V_{0}|\leq\overline{C}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
,

(iv)^{\natural}

if
H\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
, then

\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H}}(p_{H},V_{0}))\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(H)
and
\ {\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H}}(p_{H},V_{0})\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{H}
,

(iv)^{\flat}

if
H\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
, then

\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}36r_{H}}(p^{\flat}_{H},V_{0}))\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(H)
and
\ {\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}36r_{H}}(p^{\flat}_{H},V_{0})\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{H}
,

(v)^{\natural}

if
H,L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
, then

\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{H}))\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}}
and
\ {\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{L}}(p,V_{H})\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{L}
,

(v)^{\flat}

if
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
, then

\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}36r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{H}))\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}}
and
\ {\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}36r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{H})\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{L}
.

where
\Box=\
or
\Box=\flat
depending on whether the square is a boundary square or not.
Moreover,
(ii)-(v)
also hold if
H
and
L
are neighbours with
\frac{1}{2}\ell(L)\leq\ell(H)\leq\ell(L)
.

Proof.

In this proof we will use mainly the following two estimates.

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{r}(p),V)

\displaystyle=(2\pi r^{2})^{-1}\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{r}(p)}|\overset{\rightarrow}{T}(x)-\overset{\rightarrow}{V}|^{2}d\|T\|(x)

\displaystyle\leq 2(2\pi r^{2})^{-1}\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{r}(p)}|\overset{\rightarrow}{T}(x)-\overset{\rightarrow}{W}|^{2}d\|T\|(x)+C|V-W|^{2}

\displaystyle=2{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{r}(p),W)+C|V-W|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{r}(p,V),V^{\prime})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:height-estimate}}}{{\leq}}\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{r}(p,V),W)+Cr|V^{\prime}-W|,

where in the first one we used the monotonicity formula of Theorem
3.2
to see that the mass of a ball is comparable to
r^{2}
and in the second one we used the height estimate (
10.4
) of Lemma
10.4
.

We argue by induction on
i=-\log_{2}(\ell(H))
. The base step is when
i=N_{0}
and
H=L
while we pass to children squares in the induction step. By the choice of
M_{0}
and
N_{0}
, we notice that there are no squares with side length
2^{-N_{0}}
in
{\mathscr{W}}
.

The second inclusion of
(i)
, we already observed in (
10.7
) while the first inclusion of
(i)
and the inequality in
(ii)
is redundant for
H=L
. Thus, we show now
(iii)
. We use
(i)
, the optimality of
V_{H}
, the monotonicity formula of Theorem
3.2
and the definition of
{\bm{m}}_{0}
to deduce

\displaystyle|V_{H}-V_{0}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\overline{C}r_{H}^{-2}\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\Box}}|\vec{T}-\vec{V}_{H}|^{2}d\|T\|(x)+\overline{C}r_{H}^{-2}\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\Box}}|\vec{T}-\vec{V}_{0}|^{2}d\|T\|(x)

\displaystyle\leq 2\overline{C}{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\Box},V_{0})\leq\overline{C}{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{5R_{0}},V_{0})\leq\overline{C}{\bm{m}}_{0}.

(11.1)

For
(iv)
we use the height estimate (
10.4
) of Lemma
10.4
. Notice that
{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H}}(p_{H}^{\Box},V_{0})\subset{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}}(0,V_{0})
and hence,

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H}}(p_{H}^{\Box},V_{0}))\leq\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}}(0,V_{0}))\leq\overline{C}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}=\overline{C}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(H).

Then also the inclusion
{\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H}}(p^{\Box}_{H},V_{0})\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\Box}
holds, as long as
\varepsilon_{CM}
is small enough.
For
(v)
we observe that as
{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\Box}\subset{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}}(0,V_{0})
we can estimate

|p_{H}^{\Box}|^{2}\leq 9R_{0}^{2}+\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}(4R_{0},V_{0}))^{2}\leq 9R_{0}^{2}+C{\bm{m}}_{0}.

Thus if
\varepsilon_{CM}
(and thus
{\bm{m}}_{0}
) is small enough, then
{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H}}(p_{H}^{\Box},V_{H})\cap{\mathbf{B}}_{4R_{0}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}}(0,V_{0})
. Hence, also
{\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H}}(p_{H}^{\Box},V_{H})\subset{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}}(0,V_{0})
and we can estimate

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H}}(p_{H}^{\Box},V_{H}))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}}(0,V_{0}))+\overline{C}|V_{H}-V_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq\overline{C}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}=\overline{C}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(H)^{1+\beta_{1}},

where we used
(iii)
and
(iv)
.

Figure 3.
An illustration of the various relevant points in the Whitney square.

Induction step:
H\in{\mathscr{S}}_{i+1}\cup{\mathscr{W}}_{i+1}
for some
i\geq N_{0}
. Thus there is a chain of squares such that
H_{i+1}:=H\subset H_{i}\subset\cdots\subset H_{N_{0}}
with
H_{j}\in{\mathscr{S}}_{j}
for each
j\leq i
. Assume the validity of
(i)-(v)
for
H_{l}
and
H_{k}
with
N_{0}\leq l\leq k\leq i
. We want to show
(i)-(v)
for
H=H_{i+1}
and
L=H_{j}
with
N_{0}\leq j\leq i
. For
(i)
, we notice that it is enough to show the inclusion for
j=i
. Then we have
|x_{H_{i}}-x_{H}|\leq\sqrt{2}\ell(H_{i})
and hence, if
{\varepsilon}_{CM}
is small enough, we use the induction hypothesis for
(iv)
to estimate

\displaystyle|p^{\Box}_{H_{i}}-p_{H}^{\Box}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\sqrt{2}\ell(H_{i})+96r_{H_{i}})^{2}+\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{2r_{H_{i}}}(p_{H_{i}}^{\Box},V_{0}))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\ell(H_{i})^{2}(\sqrt{2}(1+96M_{0}))^{2}+C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/2}\ell(H_{i})^{2}\leq 2^{16}M_{0}^{2}\ell(H_{i})^{2}.

Now we check that
{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\Box}\subset{\mathbf{B}}^{\Box}_{H_{i}}
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle 2^{7}64r_{H}+|p^{\Box}_{H_{i}}-p_{H}^{\Box}|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{7}32\sqrt{2}M_{0}\ell(H_{i})+2^{8}M_{0}\ell(H_{i})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{7}32\sqrt{2}M_{0}\ell(H_{i})+2^{7}32\sqrt{2}M_{0}\ell(H_{i})=2^{7}64r_{H_{i}}.

For
(ii)
, we first show the special case where
j=i
. We notice that by
(i)
, the fact that
2r_{H}=r_{H_{i}}
and
H_{i}\in{\mathscr{S}}_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle|V_{H}-V_{H_{i}}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\overline{C}\frac{r_{H}^{2}}{\|T\|({\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\Box})}({\mathbf{E}}^{\Box}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\Box})+{\mathbf{E}}^{\Box}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\Box}_{H_{i}}))\qquad\textnormal{(monotonicity formula)}

\displaystyle\leq\overline{C}({\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\Box},V_{H_{i}})+{\mathbf{E}}^{\Box}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\Box}_{H_{i}}))\leq 2\overline{C}{\mathbf{E}}^{\Box}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\Box}_{H_{i}})\leq\overline{C}C_{e}^{\Box}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(H)^{2-2\delta_{1}}.

Now for a general
j\in\{N_{0},\dots,i\}
, we use the geometric series to conclude

\displaystyle|V_{H}-V_{H_{j}}|\leq\sum_{l=j}^{i}|V_{H_{l+1}}-V_{H_{l}}|

\displaystyle\leq\overline{C}C_{e}^{\Box}{\bm{m}}_{0}\sum_{l=j}^{i}\ell(H_{l})^{1-\delta_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\overline{C}C_{e}^{\Box}{\bm{m}}_{0}\sum_{l=j}^{\infty}(2^{-l+j}\ell(H_{j}))^{1-\delta_{1}}\leq\overline{C}C_{e}^{\Box}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(H_{j})^{1-\delta_{1}}.

(iii)
follows by
(ii)
and (
11
).
To prove
(iv)^{\natural}
, we observe that by the induction hypothesis, we already know
{\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H_{i}}}(p^{\Box}_{H_{i}},V_{H_{i}})\subset{\mathbf{B}}^{\Box}_{H_{i}}
. Now we want to see that
{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H}}(p_{H}^{\Box},V_{0})\subset{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H_{i}}}(p^{\Box}_{H_{i}},V_{0})
.
In case where
H_{i}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
, we have
|x_{H}-x_{H_{i}}|\leq\sqrt{2}\ell(H_{i})
, hence

36r_{H}+|x_{H}-x_{H_{i}}|\leq 36r_{H_{i}}.

On the other hand, if
H_{i}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
, then we recall
|p_{H}-p^{\flat}_{H_{i}}|\leq 2^{8}M_{0}\ell(H_{i})
which implies

36r_{H}+|x_{H}-x^{\flat}_{H_{i}}|\leq 36r_{H}+|p_{H}-p^{\flat}_{H_{i}}|\leq 2^{7}36r_{H_{i}}.

Thus the desired inclusion of the cylinders holds.
We deduce

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H}}(p_{H},V_{0}))\leq\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\Box}_{H_{i}},V_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\Box}_{H_{i}})+\overline{C}r_{H_{i}}|V_{H_{i}}-V_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(H_{i})^{1+\beta_{1}}+\overline{C}\ell(H_{i}){\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/2}\leq\overline{C}C_{h}\ell(H_{i}){\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}},

where we used the induction hypothesis and that
H_{i}\in{\mathscr{S}}_{i}
.
The previous estimate shows also that
{\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H}}(p_{H},V_{0})\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{H}
assuming that
\varepsilon_{CM}
is small enough.
The proof of
(iv)^{\flat}
is analogous because if
H\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
, then also
H_{i}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
and so as before

2^{7}36r_{H}+|x_{H}^{\flat}-x^{\flat}_{H_{i}}|\leq 2^{7}36r_{H}+|p_{H}^{\flat}-p^{\flat}_{H_{i}}|\leq 2^{7}36r_{H_{i}}.

Now we show
(v)^{\natural},(v)^{\flat}
for
H=H_{i+1}
and
L=H_{j}
for some
j\in\{N_{0},\dots,i\}
by induction on
j
. For
j=N_{0}
, we use the estimate on
|V_{H}-V_{H_{N_{0}}}|
to deduce

\Big({\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}36r_{H_{N_{0}}}}(p^{\Box}_{H_{N_{0}}},V_{H})\cap{\mathbf{B}}_{4R_{0}}\Big)\subset\Big({\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}36r_{H_{N_{0}}}}(p^{\Box}_{H_{N_{0}}},V_{H_{N_{0}}})\cap{\mathbf{B}}_{5R_{0}}\Big)\subset{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}}(0,V_{0})

provided that
\varepsilon_{CM}
is small enough. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}36r_{H_{N_{0}}}}(p^{\Box}_{H_{N_{0}}},V_{H}))\leq\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}}(0,V_{0}))+C|V_{H}-V_{0}|\leq\overline{C}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}.

Again if
\varepsilon_{CM}
is small, this also implies that
{\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}36r_{H_{N_{0}}}}(p^{\Box}_{H_{N_{0}}},V_{H}))\subset{\mathbf{B}}^{\Box}_{H_{N_{0}}}
.
Now assume that
(v)^{\natural},(v)^{\flat}
hold for some
j\geq N_{0}
and denote
L=H_{j+1}
.
We first consider the case where
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
. Then its parent
H_{j}
is still unknown, but in any case,
{\mathbf{B}}_{L}\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{H_{j}}^{\Box}
and thus,
{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{H})\subset{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H_{j}}}(p_{H_{j}},V_{H})
or
{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{H})\subset{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}36r_{H_{j}}}(p^{\flat}_{H_{j}},V_{H})
respectively. Using the induction hypothesis, we find
\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{H_{j}}}(p_{H_{j}},V_{H}))\leq\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H_{j}},V_{H})
or
\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}36r_{H_{j}}}(p^{\flat}_{H_{j}},V_{H}))\leq\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H_{j}}^{\flat},V_{H})
respectively. Moreover, using
(ii)
, we deduce

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H_{j}}^{\Box},V_{H})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H_{j}}^{\Box})+\overline{C}r_{H_{j}}|V_{H}-V_{H_{j}}|

\displaystyle\leq\overline{C}C_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\ell(H_{j})^{1+\beta_{1}}+\overline{C}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\ell(H_{j})^{2-\delta_{1}}\leq\overline{C}C_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\ell(H_{j}).

Thus, we have also
{\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{H}))\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{L}
and finally

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{36r_{L}}(p,V_{H}))\leq\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L})+\overline{C}r_{L}|V_{H}-V_{L}|\leq\overline{C}C_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}}.

On the other hand, if
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
, then also
H_{j}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
and we can perform the same argument since
{\mathbf{B}}_{L}^{\flat}\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{H_{j}}^{\flat}
and
{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}36r_{L}}(p_{L}^{\flat},V_{H})\subset{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}36r_{H_{j}}}(p^{\flat}_{H_{j}},V_{H})
. This shows both
(v)^{\natural}
and
(v)^{\flat}
.

For neighbor squares, the argument works exactly the same as everything follows from the smallness of
|p^{\Box}_{L}-p^{\Box}_{H}|
and the fact that
{\mathbf{B}}_{L}^{\Box}\cup{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\Box}\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{J}^{\Box}
, where
J
is the parent of
L
.

∎

Very similarly we now prove the excess estimates using the fact, that the parent of any square belongs to
{\mathscr{S}}
.

Proof of Proposition
10.12
.

For squares
L
of side length
2^{-N_{0}}
, we know by Proposition
11.1

(i)
that
{\mathbf{B}}_{L}^{\Box}\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{4R_{0}}
and so we can choose
C_{e}^{\natural}
and
C_{e}^{\flat}
large enough such that

{\mathbf{E}}^{\Box}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L})\leq C(R_{0},N_{0}){\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{4R_{0}},V_{0})\leq C(R_{0},N_{0}){\bm{m}}_{0}\leq C_{e}^{\Box}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(L)^{2-2\delta_{1}}.

Hence,
L\notin{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
. Similarly we see that
L\notin{\mathscr{W}}^{h}
. Indeed, we use Proposition
11.1

(ii)
and the height estimate of Lemma
10.4

\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L}^{\Box})\leq\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{4R_{0}},V_{0})+C(R_{0},n,Q)|V_{L}^{\Box}-V_{0}|\leq C(R_{0},n,Q){\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}.

Thus, we can choose
C_{h}
large enough such that
\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L}^{\Box})\leq C_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}}
. This shows (a).

We claim that (b) holds as long as
C_{e}^{\natural}\geq 16C_{e}^{\flat}
. Let
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
and assume its parent
H\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
. We want to show that
L\notin{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
. Recall that
|p_{L}-p_{H}^{\flat}|\leq 2^{8}M_{0}\ell(L)
and thus
{\mathbf{B}}_{L}\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\flat}
. Moreover, as
H
is a parent, it belongs to
{\mathscr{S}}
, thus

{\mathbf{E}}^{\flat}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\flat}_{H})\leq C_{e}^{\flat}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(H)^{2-2\delta_{1}}.

This then implies

{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L})\leq{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L},V_{H})\leq 4{\mathbf{E}}^{\flat}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\flat}_{H})\leq 16C_{e}^{\flat}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(L)^{2-2\delta_{1}}.

Now let
L\in{\mathscr{W}}\cap{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
and denote by
H\in{\mathscr{S}}
the parent of
L
. As
L
is a boundary square, so is
H
. By Proposition
11.1

(i)
and
(ii)
, we know that
{\mathbf{B}}_{L}^{\flat}\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\flat}
and

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}^{\flat}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L}^{\flat})

\displaystyle\leq 4{\mathbf{E}}^{\flat}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\flat})\leq CC_{e}^{\flat}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(L)^{2-2\delta_{1}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L}^{\flat})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\flat})+\overline{C}r_{L}|V_{L}-V_{H}|\leq CC_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}}.

On the other hand, for
L\in{\mathscr{W}}\cap{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
, the parent
H
of
L
could be either a boundary square or an interior square. So we estimate

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L})

\displaystyle\leq 4{\mathbf{E}}^{\Box}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\Box})\leq C(C_{e}^{\flat}+C_{e}^{\natural}){\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(L)^{2-2\delta_{1}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\Box})+\overline{C}r_{L}|V_{L}-V^{\Box}_{H}|\leq CC_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}}.

∎

12.
Estimates on the interpolating functions

We notice that our construction fulfills the estimates needed for the strong Lipschitz approximation.

Proposition 12.1
.

Suppose that Assumption
10.3
holds true, recall the constants from Assumption
10.7
and assume that
\varepsilon_{CM}
is small enough. Let either
H,L\in{\mathscr{S}}\cup{\mathscr{W}}
be neighbors with
\frac{1}{2}\ell(L)\leq\ell(H)\leq\ell(L)
or let
H
be a descendant of
L
. Then we have

\displaystyle{\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{32r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{H})

\displaystyle\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{L},\quad\textnormal{if }L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural},

\displaystyle{\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}32r_{L}}(p_{L}^{\flat},V_{H})

\displaystyle\subset{\mathbf{B}}_{L}^{\flat},\quad\textnormal{if }L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat},

and
[
11
, Theorem 2.4]
can be applied to
T
in the cylinder
{\mathbf{C}}_{32r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{H})
and Theorem
9.1
in
{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}32r_{L}}(p_{L}^{\flat},V_{H})
respectively. The resulting strong Lipschitz approximation we call
f_{HL}
.

Proof.

The proof of Proposition
12.1
is completely analogous to
[
13
, Proposition 4.2]
for interior squares and to
[
8
, Proposition 8.25]
for boundary squares.
∎

Remark 12.2
.

Observe that if
\ell(H)<\ell(L)
and
H
is a boundary square, then
L
is necessarily also a boundary square, since either
H
and
L
are neighbors or
H\subset L
. When
\ell(H)=\ell(L)
, in case
H
is a boundary square and
L
is an interior square, we can simply swap their roles. In particular, without loss of generality, we will in the sequel ignore the case in which
H
is a boundary square and
L
is an interior square.

Definition 12.3
.

We denote by
f_{HL}
the strong Lipschitz approximation produced by Proposition
12.1
. We will however consider the domain of the function
f_{HL}
a subset of
p_{H}+V_{H}
, resp.
p_{H}^{\flat}+V_{H}
. More precisely, for interior squares the domain is
{\mathbf{C}}_{24r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{H})\cap(p_{H}+V_{H})
, while for boundary squares it is
D_{HL}:=D_{H}\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}24r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{H})
, where we recall that
D_{H}
is the projection on
p^{\flat}_{H}+V_{H}
of
{\rm spt}(T)
. Observe that
{\mathbf{C}}_{24r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{H})\cap(p_{H}+V_{H})
and
{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}24r_{L}}(p_{L}^{\flat},V_{H})\cap(p_{H}^{\Box}+V_{H})
are discs, whose centers are given by

\displaystyle p_{HL}

\displaystyle:=p_{H}+\mathbf{p}_{V_{H}}(p_{L}),\qquad\text{resp.}

(12.1)

\displaystyle p^{\flat}_{HL}

\displaystyle:=p^{\Box}_{H}+\mathbf{p}_{V_{H}}(p_{L}^{\flat}).\,

(12.2)

(Note that, when
L
is a boundary square,
H
might be a boundary square but it might also be an interior square).

Definition 12.4
.

We then let
h_{HL}
be the harmonic function on
B_{16r_{L}}(p_{HL},V_{H})
, resp.
D_{H}\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}16r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{HL},V_{H})
, such that the boundary value of
h_{HL}
on the respective domain is given by
{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{HL}
, in particular it coincides with
g_{H}
on
\gamma_{H}
.
h_{HL}
will be called the
(H,L)
-tilted harmonic interpolating function
.

Lemma
10.15
will then be a particular case of the following more general lemma.

Lemma 12.5
.

Consider
H
and
L
as in Proposition
12.1
. Then there is a smooth function
u_{HL}:D\cap B_{2^{7}8r_{L}}({\mathbf{p}}_{0}(p^{\flat}_{L}),V_{0})\to V_{0}^{\perp}
, resp.
u_{HL}:B_{8r_{L}}({\mathbf{p}}_{0}(p_{L}),V_{0})\to V_{0}^{\perp}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{u_{HL}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{8r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{G}_{h_{HL}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{8r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{0}),

(12.3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{u_{HL}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}8r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{G}_{h_{HL}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}8r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{0}),\quad\textnormal{respectively}.

(12.4)

The function
u_{HL}
will be called
interpolating function
.

12.1.
Linearization and first estimates on
h_{HL}

Proposition 12.6
.

Under the Assumptions of Proposition
12.1
the following estimates hold for every pair of squares
H
and
L
as in Proposition
12.1
. First of all

\displaystyle\int D({\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{HL}):D\zeta\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}r_{L}^{4+\beta_{1}}\|D\zeta\|_{0},

(12.5)

for every function
\zeta
in
C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{8r_{L}}(p_{HL},V_{H}),V_{H}^{\perp})
, resp.
C^{\infty}_{c}(D_{H}\cap B_{2^{7}8r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{HL},V_{H}),V_{H}^{\perp})
, depending on whether
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
or
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\|h_{HL}-{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{HL}\|_{L^{1}(B_{8r_{L}}(p_{HL},V_{H}))}

\displaystyle\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}r_{L}^{5+\beta_{1}},\qquad

if
L\in\mathscr{C}^{\natural}
;

(12.6)

\displaystyle\|h_{HL}-{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{HL}\|_{L^{1}(D_{H}\cap B_{2^{7}8r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{HL},V_{H}))}

\displaystyle\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}r_{L}^{5+\beta_{1}},\qquad

if
L\in\mathscr{C}^{\flat}
;

(12.7)

\displaystyle\|Dh_{HL}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{7r_{L}}(p_{HL},V_{H}))}

\displaystyle\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}r_{L}^{1-\delta_{1}},\qquad

if
L\in\mathscr{C}^{\natural}
;

(12.8)

\displaystyle\|Dh_{HL}\|_{L^{\infty}(D_{H}\cap B_{2^{7}7r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{HL},V_{H}))}

\displaystyle\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}r_{L}^{1-\delta_{1}},\qquad

if
L\in\mathscr{C}^{\flat}
.

(12.9)

Proof.

Proof of (
12.5
).
Without loss of generality consider a system of coordinates
(x,y)
with the property that
p^{\Box}_{HL}
is the origin,
(x,0)\in V_{H}
and
(0,y)\in V_{H}^{\perp}
. Fix
\zeta
as in the statement of the proposition and in the cylinder
{\mathbf{C}}\in\{{\mathbf{C}}_{32r_{L}}(p_{HL},V_{H}),{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}32r_{L}}(p_{HL}^{\flat},V_{H})\}
we consider the vector field
\chi(x,y)=(0,\zeta(x))
. Observe that, by assumption, the vector field vanishes on
\Gamma
. Observe that, though
\chi
is not compactly supported, since the height of the current in the cylinder
{\mathbf{C}}
is bounded, we can multiply
\chi
by a cut-off function in the variable
y
but keeping its values the same on
{\rm spt}(T)
. The latter vector field is a valid first variation for the area-minimizing current
T
and thus we have
\delta T(\chi)=0
. Thus we can use Theorem
9.1
and Proposition
11.1
to estimate

\displaystyle|\delta\mathbf{G}_{f_{HL}}(\chi)|

\displaystyle=|\delta(T-\mathbf{G}_{f_{HL}})(\chi)|\leq\|D\zeta\|_{0}\|T-\mathbf{G}_{f_{HL}}\|({\mathbf{C}})

\displaystyle\leq C\|D\zeta\|_{0}r_{L}^{2}({\mathbf{E}}^{\Box}(T,{\mathbf{C}},V_{H})+\mathbf{A}^{2}r_{L}^{2})^{1+\gamma_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|D\zeta\|_{0}r_{L}^{2}({\mathbf{E}}^{\Box}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\Box}_{L})+|V_{H}-V_{L}|^{2}+\mathbf{A}^{2}r_{L}^{2})^{1+\gamma_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|D\zeta\|_{0}r_{L}^{2}({\bm{m}}_{0}r_{L}^{2-2\delta_{1}})^{1+\gamma_{1}}\leq C\|D\zeta\|_{0}{\bm{m}}_{0}r_{L}^{4+\beta_{1}}\,,

provided
\delta_{1}
and
\beta_{1}
are chosen small enough to satisfy
(2-2\delta_{1})(1+\gamma_{1})\geq 2+\beta_{1}
.

Next we use the Taylor expansion
[
12
, Theorem 4.1]
to estimate

\displaystyle\left|\delta\mathbf{G}_{f_{HL}}(\chi)-Q\int{\bm{\eta}}\circ Df_{HL}:D\zeta\right|\leq C\|D\zeta\|_{0}\int|Df_{HL}|^{3}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|D\zeta\|_{0}{\rm{Lip}}(f_{HL})\int|Df_{HL}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|D\zeta\|_{0}({\mathbf{E}}^{\Box}(T,{\mathbf{C}},V_{H})+\mathbf{A}^{2}r_{L}^{2})^{\gamma_{1}}r_{L}^{2}({\mathbf{E}}^{\Box}(T,{\mathbf{C}},V_{H})+\mathbf{A}^{2}r_{L}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|D\zeta\|_{0}r_{L}^{2}({\bm{m}}_{0}r_{L}^{2-2\delta_{1}})^{1+\gamma_{1}}\,.

Proof of (
12.6
)-(
12.7
).
Consider
v:=h_{HL}-{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{HL}
on its respective domain
\Omega
which equals either
B_{8r_{L}}(p_{HL},V_{H})
or
D_{H}\cap B_{2^{7}8r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{HL},V_{H})
. Observe that
v
vanishes on the boundary of
\Omega
. For every
w\in L^{2}
we denote by
\zeta=P(w)
the unique solution of
\Delta\zeta=w
in
\Omega
with
\zeta|_{\partial\Omega}=0
, which is an element of the Sobolev space
W^{1,2}_{0}(\Omega)
. Next notice that by a simple density argument, the estimate (
12.5
) remains valid for any test function
\zeta\in W^{1,2}_{0}
and recall also the standard estimate

\|D(P(w))\|_{0}\leq Cr\|w\|_{0}\,.

Therefore we can write

\displaystyle\|v\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{w:\|w\|_{0}\leq 1}\int_{\Omega}v\cdot w=\sup_{w:\|w\|_{0}\leq 1}\int_{\Omega}v\cdot\Delta(P(w))

\displaystyle=\sup_{w:\|w\|_{0}\leq 1}\left(-\int_{\Omega}Dv:D(P(w))\right)=\sup_{w:\|w\|_{0}\leq 1}\int_{\Omega}D{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{HL}:D(P(w))

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{w:\|w\|_{0}\leq 1}{\bm{m}}_{0}r_{L}^{4+\beta_{1}}\|DP(w)\|_{0}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}r_{L}^{5+\beta_{1}}\,.

Proof of (
12.8
).
Using the mean-value inequality for harmonic functions we simply get

\displaystyle\|Dh_{HL}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{7r_{L}}(p_{HL},V_{H}))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{r_{L}^{2}}\int_{B_{8r_{L}}(p_{HL},V_{H})}|Dh_{HL}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{r_{L}}\left(\int_{B_{8r_{L}}(p_{HL},V_{H})}|Dh_{HL}|^{2}\right)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{r_{L}}\left(\int_{B_{8r_{L}}(p_{HL},V_{H})}|D{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{HL}|^{2}\right)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{r_{L}}\left(r_{L}^{2}({\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{C}},V_{H})+\mathbf{A}^{2}r_{L}^{2})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}r_{L}^{1-\delta_{1}}\,.

Proof of (
12.9
).
Using standard Schauder estimates for harmonic functions, we get

\displaystyle\|Dh_{HL}\|_{L^{\infty}(D_{H}\cap B_{2^{7}7r_{L}}(p_{HL}^{\flat},V_{H}))}\leq\frac{C}{r_{L}^{2}}\int_{D_{H}\cap B_{2^{7}8r_{L}}(p_{HL}^{\flat},V_{H})}|Dh_{HL}|+C(\|Dg_{H}\|_{0}+r_{L}^{-\alpha}[g_{H}]_{\alpha})\,,

where we recall that
g_{H}:\partial D_{H}\cap B_{2^{7}8r_{L}}(p_{HL}^{\flat},V_{H})
is the graphical parametrization of our boundary curve
\Gamma
and
\alpha
is a positive number smaller than
1
, to be chosen later. The first summand on the right hand side is estimated as in the proof above of (
12.8
). As for the second summand, recall that
T_{p^{\flat}_{L}}\Gamma
is contained in the plane
V_{L}
and that
|V_{L}-V_{H}|\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{L}^{1-\delta_{1}}
. This implies that

|Dg_{H}(p^{\flat}_{HL})|\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{L}^{1-\delta_{1}}\,.

In particular we have

\|Dg_{H}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial D_{H}\cap B_{2^{7}8r_{L}}(p_{HL}^{\flat},V_{H}))}\leq|Dg_{H}(p^{\flat}_{HL})|+C\mathbf{A}r_{L}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{L}^{1-\delta_{1}}\,.

On the other hand,

r_{L}^{-\alpha}[g_{H}]_{\alpha}\leq Cr_{L}^{1-2\alpha}\mathbf{A}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{L}^{1-2\alpha}\,,

and thus it suffices to choose
2\alpha<\delta_{1}
.
∎

12.2.
Tilted estimate

We follow here
[
8
, Section 8.5]
almost verbatim to establish a suitable comparison between tilted interpolating functions which are defined in different system of coordinates.

Definition 12.7
.

Four cubes
H,J,L,M\in\mathscr{C}
make a
distant relation
between
H
and
L
if
J,M
are neighbors (possibly the same cube) with same side length and
H
and
L
are descendants respectively of
J
and
M
.

Lemma 12.8

(Tilted
L^{1}
estimate)
.

Under the Assumptions of Theorem
10.16
the following holds for every quadruple
H,J,L
and
M
in
{\mathscr{S}}\cup{\mathscr{W}}
which makes a distant relation between
H
and
L
.

•

If
J\in\mathscr{C}^{\natural}
, then there is a map
\hat{h}_{LM}:B_{4r_{J}}(p_{HJ},V_{H})\to V_{H}^{\perp}
such that

\mathbf{G}_{\hat{h}_{LM}}=\mathbf{G}_{h_{LM}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{4r_{J}}(p_{HJ},V_{H})

and

\|h_{HJ}-\hat{h}_{LM}\|_{L^{1}(B_{2r_{J}}(p_{HJ},V_{H}))}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(J)^{5+\beta_{1}/2}\,.

(12.10)

•

If both
J
and
M
belong to
\mathscr{C}^{\flat}
, then there is a map
\hat{h}_{LM}:D_{HJ}\cap B_{2^{7}4r_{J}}(p^{\flat}_{HJ},V_{H})\to V_{H}^{\perp}
such that

\mathbf{G}_{\hat{h}_{LM}}=\mathbf{G}_{h_{LM}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}4r_{J}}(p^{\flat}_{HJ},V_{H})

and

\|h_{HJ}-\hat{h}_{LM}\|_{L^{1}(D_{HJ}\cap B_{2^{7}2r_{J}}(p^{\flat}_{HJ},V_{H}))}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(J)^{5+\beta_{1}/2}\,.

(12.11)

The proof follows verbatim the arguments given in
[
8
, Section 8.5]
. The only difference is the absence of the “ambient Riemannian” manifold which in
[
8
, Lemma 8.31]
is the graph of a function
\Psi
. The case needed for our arguments is the clearly simpler situation in which the linear subspaces
\varpi
and
\bar{\varpi}
in
[
8
, Lemma 8.31]
are given by the trivial subspace
\{0\}
. The proof of this version of the lemma (which is in fact
[
13
, Lemma 5.6]
) is even less complicated. However there is a direct way to conclude it directly from the more general statement of
[
8
, Lemma 8.31]
: we can consider
\mathbb{R}^{2+n}
as a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{2+n+1}
and apply
[
8
, Lemma 8.31]
to a generic choice of
\varkappa,\bar{\varkappa},\pi,\bar{\pi}
and the specific choice of
\varpi=\bar{\varpi}=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\Psi=\bar{\Psi}:\pi\times\varkappa=\bar{\pi}\times\bar{\varkappa}\to\varpi=\bar{\varpi}
given by the trivial map
\Psi\equiv 0
.

13.
Final estimates and proof of Theorem
10.16

Proposition 13.1
.

There is a constant
\omega
depending upon
\delta_{1}
and
\beta_{1}
such that,
under the assumptions of Theorem
10.16
, the following holds for every pair of squares
H,L\in\mathscr{P}^{j}
(cf. (
10.13
)).

(a)

\|u_{H}\|_{C^{3,\omega}(B_{4r_{H}}(x_{H})}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/2}
, resp.
\|u_{H}\|_{C^{3,\omega}(D\cap B_{2^{7}4r_{H}}(x^{\flat}_{H}))}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/2}
, for
H\in\mathscr{C}^{\natural}
, resp.
H\in\mathscr{C}^{\flat}
;

(b)

If
H
and
L
are neighbors then for any
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, we have

\displaystyle\|u_{H}-u_{L}\|_{C^{i}(B_{r_{H}}(x_{H}))}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/2}\ell(H)^{3+\omega-i}\qquad\mbox{when $H\in\mathscr{C}^{\natural}$,}

(13.1)

\displaystyle\|u_{H}-u_{L}\|_{C^{i}(D\cap B_{2^{7}r_{H}}(x^{\flat}_{H}))}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/2}\ell(H)^{3+\omega-i}\qquad\mbox{when $H,L\in\mathscr{C}^{\flat}$;}

(13.2)

(c)

|D^{3}u_{H}(x_{H}^{\Box})-D^{3}u_{L}(x^{\Box}_{L})|\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/2}|x^{\square}_{H}-x^{\square}_{L}|^{\omega}
, where
\square=\phantom{\flat}
if the corresponding square is a non-boundary square and
\square=\flat
if it is a boundary square;

(d)

if
H\in\mathscr{C}^{\natural}
, then
\|u_{H}-{\mathbf{p}}_{V_{0}}^{\perp}(p_{H})\|_{C^{0}(B_{4r_{H}}(x_{H}))}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/2}\ell(H)
and if
H\in\mathscr{C}^{\flat}
, then
u_{H}|_{\partial D\cap B_{2^{7}4r_{H}}(x_{H}^{\flat}))}=g
;

(e)

|V_{H}-T_{(x,u_{H}(x))}\mathbf{G}_{u_{H}}|\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/2}\ell(H)^{1-\delta_{1}}
for every
x\in B_{4r_{H}}(x_{H})
, resp.
x\in D\cap B_{2^{7}4r_{H}}(x^{\flat}_{H})
;

(f)

If
H^{\prime}
is the square concentric to
H\in{\mathscr{W}}_{j}
with
\ell(H^{\prime})=\frac{9}{8}\ell(H)
, then

\|{\bm{\varphi}}_{i}-u_{H}\|_{L^{1}(H^{\prime})}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(H)^{5+\beta_{1}/2}\qquad\forall i\geq j+1\,.

(13.3)

13.1.
Proof of Proposition
13.1

Proof.

We follow the proof of
[
8
, Proposition 8.32]
and often we drop here for simplicity the domains where we estimate the norm in.

By
[
11
, Lemma B.1]
, it is enough to make the estimates on
h_{H}
instead of
u_{H}
. Fix any square
H\in{\mathscr{P}}^{j}
and consider the family tree
H=H_{i}\subset H_{i-1}\subset\cdots\subset H_{N_{0}}
. We estimate

\|h_{H}\|_{C^{3,\omega}}\leq\sum_{j=N_{0}+1}^{i}\|h_{HH_{j}}-h_{HH_{j-1}}\|_{C^{3,\omega}}+\|h_{HH_{N_{0}}}\|_{C^{3,\omega}}\,.

As these are all harmonic functions, by the mean value property, it is enough to estimate the
L^{1}
norms. Again using the harmonicity we see that

\|h_{HH_{j}}-h_{HH_{j-1}}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{j})}\leq\|{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{HH_{j}}-{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{HH_{j-1}}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{j})}+C{\bm{m}}_{0}r_{H_{j-1}}^{5+\beta_{1}},

where
\Omega_{j}
either is
B_{7r_{H_{j}}}(p_{H_{j}},V_{H})
if
H_{j}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
or
D_{H}\cap B_{2^{7}7r_{H_{j}}}(p^{\flat}_{H_{j}},V_{H})
if
H_{j}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
. Using Theorem
9.1
, we see that both
f_{HH_{j}}
and
f_{HH_{j-1}}
describe
{\rm spt}(T)
on a large set
K
, thus their average agree on
K
. Together with the oscillation estimate we then deduce

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{HH_{j}}-{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{HH_{j-1}}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{j})}

\displaystyle\leq C\ell(H_{j-1})^{2}\left({\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(H_{j-1})^{2-2\delta_{1}}\right)^{1+\gamma_{1}}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\ell(H_{j-1})^{1+\beta_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(H_{j-1})^{5+\beta_{1}}\,.

For
\|h_{HH_{N_{0}}}\|_{C^{3,\omega}}
we argue similarly and use Proposition
12.6
.

By
[
11
, Lemma C.2]
, we have

\|D^{j}(u_{H}-u_{L})\|_{C^{0}}\leq CCr_{L}^{-2-j}\|u_{H}-u_{L}\|_{L^{1}}+Cr_{L}^{3+\omega-j}\|D^{3}(u_{H}-u_{L})\|_{C^{\omega}}.

The second term is already bounded in (a), thus we are left with showing the
L^{1}
estimate. To do so, we again use
[
11
, Lemma B.1]
to replace
u_{L}
and
u_{H}
with functions which have the same graph. It is enough to notice that, by Lemma
12.8

\|h_{H}-\hat{h}_{L}\|_{L^{1}}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\ell(H)^{5+\delta_{1}/2}.

Let
H,L\in\mathscr{P}^{j}
. In case that
|x_{H}-x_{L}|\geq 2^{-N_{0}}
, the statement follows from (a). Otherwise, we can find ancestors
J,M
such that
H,L
are in a distant relation where
\ell(J)=\ell(M)
is comparable to
|x_{H}^{\Box}-x_{L}^{\Box}|
. Then we estimate

\displaystyle|D^{3}u_{H}(x_{H}^{\Box})-D^{3}u_{L}(x^{\Box}_{L})|

\displaystyle\leq|D^{3}u_{H}(x_{H}^{\Box})-D^{3}u_{HJ}(x^{\Box}_{J})|+|D^{3}u_{LM}(x_{M}^{\Box})-D^{3}u_{L}(x^{\Box}_{L})|

\displaystyle\quad+|D^{3}u_{HJ}(x_{J}^{\Box})-D^{3}u_{LM}(x^{\Box}_{M})|.

The bound on the last term is already shown in (b), while for the first two we argue similarly as before. Consider the family tree
H\subset H_{i-1}\subset\cdots\subset J
. By the previous arguments, we deduce

\|u_{HH_{i}}-u_{HH_{i-1}}\|_{C^{3}}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\ell(H_{i-1})^{\omega}.

The claim is obvious by construction for boundary cubes. For non-boundary cubes, consider that the height bound for
T
and the Lipschitz regularity for
f_{H}
give that

\left\|\mathbf{p}_{V_{H}}^{\perp}\left(p_{H}\right)-\bm{\eta}\circ f_{H}\right\|_{\infty}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(H).

We also get

\left\|\mathbf{p}_{V_{H}}^{\perp}\left(p_{H}\right)-{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{H}\right\|_{\infty}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(H)
. On the other hand the Lipschitz
regularity of the tilted
H
-interpolating function
h_{H}
and the
L^{1}
estimate on
h_{H}-{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{H}
easily gives
\left\|\mathbf{p}_{V_{H}}^{\perp}\left(p_{H}\right)-h_{H}\right\|_{\infty}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(H)
. The
estimate claimed in (d) follows then from
[
13
, Lemma B.1]
.

follows from the estimates on
D\overline{h}_{HL}
of Lemma
12.8
.

By definition of
{\bm{\varphi}}_{j}
, it is enough to estimate that for
L
a neighbour square of
H
, we have

\|u_{H}-u_{L}\|_{L^{1}}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(H)^{5+\delta_{1}/2}.

∎

13.2.
Proof of Theorem
10.16

Proof.

(a) is an immediate consequence of the definition of

{\bm{\varphi}}_{j}
and the fact that
u_{L}
satisfies the correct boundary condition (for
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}
).
(b) follows exactly as in the proof of
[
11
, Theorem 1.17]
and from Proposition
13.1
. In fact, we are in the simpler situation where our ”ambient manifold” is just
\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
and thus, we can choose
\Psi\equiv 0
. (c) and (d) are consequences of (b).
∎

13.3.
Proof of Corollary
10.19
and Theorem
10.21

Proof.

We extend
{\bm{\varphi}}
to all of
[-4,4]^{2}
changing the
C^{3,\omega}
-norm only by geometric constant and call this extension
\tilde{\bm{\varphi}}
. Then consider

\tilde{T}:=T+Q\cdot\mathbf{G}_{\tilde{\bm{\varphi}}|_{[-4,4]^{2}\setminus D}}.

Then as
\partial\mathcal{M}=\Gamma
, so
\partial\tilde{T}=0
. We cannot directly apply the corresponding interior paper,
[
13
, Corollary 2.2]
, to
\tilde{T}
because the latter is not area-minimizing. However, the argument given in
[
13
, Proof of Corollary 2.2]
does not use the area-minimizing assumption. It uses only the height estimates of Proposition
11.1
(which can be trivially extended to
\tilde{T}
since the portion added to
T
is regular) and the constancy theorem (which is valid in our case, since
\tilde{T}
has no boundary).

As for the existence and estimates on the normal approximation, we also can follow the same argument as in
[
13
, Section 6.2]
substituting the current
\tilde{T}
to the current
T
in there and the map
\tilde{\bm{\varphi}}
to the map
{\bm{\varphi}}
in there. First of all notice that the extension is done locally on each square and the ones surrounding it, and thus, even though the union of the squares in our
{\mathscr{W}}
and the set
\mathbf{\Delta}
does not cover
[-4,4]^{2}
, this does not prevent us from applying the same procedure. Next, the construction algorithm and the estimates performed in
[
13
, Section 6.2]
depend only on the following two facts:

(a)

The map
{\bm{\varphi}}
in
[
13
, Section 6.2]
has, on every
L\in{\mathscr{W}}
, the same control on the
C^{3,\omega}
norm that we have for the map
\tilde{\bm{\varphi}}
(up to a constant).

(b)

For each square
L\in{\mathscr{W}}
(which in the case of
[
13
, Section 6.2]
corresponds to an interior square for us) we have a Lipschitz approximation
f_{L}
of the current
T\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{8r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{L})
, which in turn coincides with the current
T
on a set
K_{L}\times V_{L}^{\perp}
, where
|B_{8r_{L}}\setminus K_{L}|
is small and the Lipschitz constant and the height of
f_{L}
are both suitably small too. This is literally the case with the very same estimates for our interior squares, because
\tilde{T}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{8r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{L})=T\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{8r_{L}}(p_{L},V_{L})
. In the case of boundary squares, we apply Theorem
9.1
and we extend the corresponding
f_{L}
to a map
\tilde{F}_{L}
on the whole disk
B_{2^{7}8r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{L})
by setting it equal to
Q
copies of the graph of
\tilde{\bm{\varphi}}
outside of the domain
D_{L}\cap B_{2^{7}8r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{L})
. We then notice that such extension satisfies the same estimates on the Lipschitz constant and the height. Moreover, over the new region, by construction the extension coincides with the current
T
. Hence, if we denote by
\tilde{K}_{L}
the complement of the projection on
V_{L}
of the difference set
{\rm spt}(\tilde{T})\Delta{\rm spt}(\mathbf{G}_{L}(f_{L}))
, then

B_{2^{7}8r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{L})\setminus\tilde{K}_{L}=(B_{2^{7}8r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{L})\cap D_{L})\setminus K_{L}\,.

In particular
|B_{2^{7}8r_{L}}(p^{\flat}_{L},V_{L})\setminus\tilde{K}_{L}|
has the desired estimate.

Finally, observe the following. By the construction of
[
13
, Section 6.2]
we have a specific description of the set
\mathcal{K}
consistsing of those points
p
in the center manifold for which we know that the slice
\langle T,\mathbf{p},p\rangle
coincides with the slice of the multivalued approximation, namely
\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{F_{i}(p)}\right\rrbracket
. First of all,
\mathcal{K}
contains
{\bm{\Phi}}(\mathbf{\Delta})
. Secondly, for every Whitney region
{\mathcal{L}}
corresponding to some square
L\in{\mathscr{W}}
,
\mathcal{K}\cap{\mathcal{L}}
is defined in the following fashion. First of all, we denote by
\mathscr{D}(L)
the family of squares
M\in{\mathscr{W}}
which have nonempty intersection with
L
(i.e. its neighbors), hence we consider in each
{\mathbf{C}}_{M}:={\mathbf{C}}_{8r_{M}}(p_{M},V_{M})
, resp.
{\mathbf{C}}_{M}:={\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}8r_{M}}(p_{M},V_{M})
, the corresponding Lipschitz approximation
f_{L}
and define

\mathcal{K}\cap{\mathcal{L}}:=\bigcap_{M\in\mathscr{D}(L)}\mathbf{p}({\rm spt}(T)\cap{\rm gr}\,(f_{M}))\,.

Since for boundary cubes
\Gamma\cap{\mathbf{C}}_{M}\subset{\rm spt}(T)\cap{\rm gr}\,(f_{M})
, we conclude that
\Gamma\cap{\mathcal{L}}\subset\mathcal{K}
. On the other hand every point of
\Gamma\cap{\mathcal{M}}
which does not belong to some Whitney region is necessarily contained in the contact set
{\bm{\Phi}}(\mathbf{\Delta})
. Thus we conclude that
\Gamma\subset\mathcal{K}
. Observe, moreover, that by construction the map
N
vanishes identically on the contact set, while we also know that for each
f_{M}
as above
f_{M}
coincides with the function
g_{M}
on
\mathbf{p}_{V_{M}}(\Gamma)
. In particular this implies that
N
vanishes identically on the intersection of
\Gamma
with any Whitney region.
∎

13.4.
Proof of Proposition
10.23

(
10.24
) is an ovious consequence of (
10.23
) since on the complement of the squares
L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
the two functions
{\bm{\varphi}}
and
f
coincide.

We now turn to (
10.23
)
Observe next that, by Proposition
13.1
(f), it suffices to show the claim for the function
u_{H}
in place of
{\bm{\varphi}}
. Observe also that we already know from the above argument that, if we replace
u_{H}
with the tilted interpolating function
h_{H}
and
f
with the Lipschitz approximation
f_{H}=f_{HH}
, the estimate holds, as it is in fact just a special case of (
12.7
) and (
12.6
). Fix now a point
x\in H
and the corresponding point let
y(x):={\mathbf{p}}_{V_{H}}(u_{H}(x))
be the corresponding projection on the plane
V_{H}
. We can use
[
13
, (5.4)]
(where we identify the manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
in there with the affine plane
V_{H}+{\bm{\varphi}}(p)
) to compute

|{\bm{\eta}}\circ f(x)-u_{H}(x)|\leq C|{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{H}(y)-h_{H}(y)|+C|V_{H}-V_{0}|{\rm{Lip}}(f)\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H})\,.

In particular we conclude

|{\bm{\eta}}\circ f(x)-u_{H}(x)|\leq C|{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{H}(y)-h_{H}(y)|+C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/2}\ell(H)^{1-\delta_{1}}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\gamma_{2}}\ell(L)^{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/4}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}}\,.

Observing that
x\mapsto y(x)
is a Lipschitz function with Lipschitz constant bounded by
|D{\bm{\varphi}}|
, i.e. by
C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1/2}
and integrating in
x
, we easily conclude the claimed estimate.

14.
Local lower bounds for the Dirichlet energy and the
L^{2}
norm of
N

As in
[
13
, Section 3]
the aim of this section is to conclude suitable lower bounds for
\int|DN|^{2}
and
|N|
over regions of the center manifold which are close (and sizable) enough to some Whitney region
\mathcal{L}
. Depending on the reason why the refinement was stopped, we will either bound
|N|
from below in terms of
\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}}
or we will bound
\int|DN|^{2}
from below in terms of the excess of the current in
{\mathbf{B}}_{L}

14.1.
Lower bound on
|N|

We start with the following conclusion.

Proposition 14.1

(Separation because of the height)
.

If
L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{h}
then
L
is necessarily an interior square. Moreover, there is constant

\tilde{C}>0
depending on
M_{0}
such that whenever
(C_{h})^{4}\geq\tilde{C}C_{e}^{\natural}
and
{\varepsilon}_{CM}>0
is small enough, then every
L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{h}
fulfills

(S1)

\Theta(T,p)\leq Q-\frac{1}{2}
for all
p\in{\mathbf{B}}_{16r_{L}}(p_{L})
,

(S2)

L\cap H=\emptyset
for all
H\in{\mathscr{W}}^{n}
with
\ell(H)\leq\frac{1}{2}\ell(L)
,

(S3)

{\mathcal{G}}(N(x),Q\left\llbracket{{\bm{\eta}}\circ N(x)}\right\rrbracket)\geq\frac{1}{4}C_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\ell(L)^{1+\beta_{1}}
for all
x\in{\bm{\Phi}}(B_{2\sqrt{2}\ell(L)}(x_{L}))
.

Proof.

We only have to prove that
L\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
as the rest follows from the interior theory in
[
13
, Section 3]
. We show that any boundary square
H
which did not stop because of the excess, also did not stop because of the height. Fix such an
H\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}\setminus{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
. Then we know that its parent
M\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\flat}\cap{\mathscr{S}}
satisfies

{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{M}^{\flat})\leq C_{e}^{\flat}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(H)^{2-2\delta_{1}}

and we want to show that

\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\flat})\leq C_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\ell(H)^{1+\beta_{1}}.

To do so, we apply the height bound of Lemma
10.4
to a suitable rotated current
\tilde{T}:=O_{\sharp}T
, where
O
is a rotation which maps
V_{0}
onto
V_{H}
. Notice that the proof of this lemma is based on the first variation and thus on the minimality of
T
. As
\tilde{T}
is area minimizing (with respect to the tilted boundary
O(\Gamma)
), we can directly deduce

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{H}^{\flat})\leq\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}64r_{H}}(p_{H}^{\flat},V_{H}))

\displaystyle\leq Cr_{H}\big({\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{2^{7}80r_{H}}(p_{H}^{\flat},V_{H})+\mathbf{A}r_{H}\big)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq Cr_{H}\big({\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{M}^{\flat})+C|V_{M}-V_{H}|^{2}+\mathbf{A}r_{H}\big)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{H}^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{h}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\ell(H)^{1+\beta_{1}},

where we also used Proposition
11.1
and the sufficient small choice of
{\varepsilon}_{CM}
.
∎

A simple corollary of the above proposition is that if a square stopped because of the neighbor condition, then this originated from a larger nearby square which stopped because of the excess.

Corollary 14.2
.

For every
H\in{\mathscr{W}}^{n}
, there is a chain of squares
L_{0},L_{1},\dots,L_{j}=H
such that

(a)

L_{i}\in{\mathscr{W}}^{n}
for all
i>0
and
L_{0}\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
,

(b)

they are all neighbors, i.e.
L_{i}\cap L_{i-1}\neq\emptyset
and
\ell(L_{i})=\frac{1}{2}\ell(L_{i-1})
.

In particular,
H\subset B_{3\sqrt{2}\ell(L_{0})}(x_{L_{0}},V_{0})
.

Accordingly, we can collect all the squares
H
which have such a chain relating
H
to a specific square
L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
. The latter square is not necessarily unique, but it will be convenient to fix a consistent choice of
L
.

Definition 14.3

(Domains of influence)
.

First, let us fix an ordering
\{J_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of
{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
such that the side length is non-increasing. For
J_{0}
, we define its domain of influence by

{\mathscr{W}}^{n}(J_{0}):=\{H\in{\mathscr{W}}^{n}:\textnormal{there is a chain as in Corollary }\ref{c:chain_of_influence}\textnormal{ with }L_{0}=J_{0}\textnormal{ and }L_{j}=H\}.

Inductively, we define for
k>0
the domain of influence
{\mathscr{W}}^{n}(J_{k})
of
J_{k}
by all
H\in{\mathscr{W}}^{n}\setminus\bigcup_{i<k}{\mathscr{W}}^{n}(J_{i})
which have a chain as in Corollary
14.2
with
L_{0}=J_{k}
and
L_{j}=H
. As it is easy to check using Corollary
14.2
we have
{\mathscr{W}}^{n}=\mathring{\bigcup}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathscr{W}}^{n}(J_{k})
.

14.2.
Lower bound on the Dirichlet energy

Having handled the case of “height stopped” squares we turn to squares which were stopped because they exceed the excess bound.

Proposition 14.4
.

(Splitting)
There are constants
C_{1}(\delta_{1}),C_{2}(M_{0},\delta_{1})
,
C_{3}(M_{0},\delta_{1})
such that, if
M_{0}\geq C_{1}(\delta_{1})
,
C^{\natural}_{e}\geq C_{2}(M_{0},\delta_{1})
,
C^{\flat}_{e}\geq C_{3}(M_{0},\delta_{1})
, if
the hypotheses of Theorem
10.21
hold and if
{\varepsilon}_{CM}
is chosen sufficiently small,
then the following holds. If
L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
,
q\in V_{0}
with
{\rm{dist}}(L,q)\leq 4\sqrt{2}\,\ell(L)
,
B_{\ell(L)/4}(q,V_{0})\subset D
and
\Omega={\bm{\Phi}}(B_{\ell(L)/4}(q,V_{0}))
, then (with
C,C_{4}=C(\beta_{1},\delta_{1},M_{0},N_{0},C^{\natural}_{e},C^{\flat}_{e},C_{h})
):

\displaystyle C_{e}^{\Box}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(L)^{4-2\delta_{1}}\leq\ell(L)^{2}{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\Box}_{L})\leq C\int_{\Omega}|DN|^{2}\,,

(14.1)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{L}}}|DN|^{2}\leq C\ell(L)^{2}{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\Box}_{L})\leq C_{4}\ell(L)^{-2}\int_{\Omega}|N|^{2}\,.

(14.2)

Before coming to the proof of the Proposition, let us first observe an important point. Fix
L
as in the statement of the Proposition and consider its parent
H
and its ancestor
J

6
generations before. If
L
is a boundary square, then
H
and
J
are both boundary squares. On the other hand, if
L
is an interior square, since
C_{e}^{\natural}
is chosen much larger than
