C_{e}^{\flat}
, we can ensure that both
L
and
J
are also interior squares. Indeed, when
{\mathbf{B}}_{L}\subset{\mathbf{B}}^{\flat}_{J}
and
J\not\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
, we have the obvious estimate

{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{L})\leq 2^{26}{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}^{\flat}_{J})\leq 2^{26}C^{\flat}_{e}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(J)^{2-2\delta_{1}}\leq 2^{38}C^{\flat}_{e}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell(L)^{2-2\delta_{1}}\,,

which therefore, by choosing
C^{\natural}_{e}\geq 2^{38}C^{\flat}_{e}
implies that
L
does not satisfy the excess stopping condition.

Hence we can invoke
[
13
, Proposition 3.4]
to cover the case in which
L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}\cap{\mathscr{C}}^{\natural}
, since the proof given in
[
13
, Section 7.3]
just uses the fact that all squares
L,H
and
J
are interior squares (i.e. the repsective balls
{\mathbf{B}}_{L},{\mathbf{B}}_{H}
, and
{\mathbf{B}}_{J}
do not intersect the boundary
\Gamma
). We are thus left to handle the case in which
L
(and therefore also
H
and
J
) are boundary squares.

To do so, we need analogues of three lemmas from
[
13
]
.

Lemma 14.5
.

Let
B^{+}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be a half ball centered at the origin and
w\in W^{1,2}(B^{+},{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}))
be Dir-minimizing with
w=Q\left\llbracket{0}\right\rrbracket
on
B^{+}\cap({\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\})
. Denoting
\bar{w}:=w\oplus(-{\bm{\eta}}\circ w)=\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{w_{i}-{\bm{\eta}}\circ w}\right\rrbracket
and
u:={\bm{\eta}}\circ w
, we have

\displaystyle Q\int_{B^{+}}|Du-Du(0)|^{2}=\int_{B^{+}}{\mathcal{G}}(Dw,Q\left\llbracket{Du(0)}\right\rrbracket)^{2}-\textup{Dir}(\bar{w},B^{+}).

Proof.

We extend
w
in an odd way to all of the ball
B
. Notice that then also the extension of
u
is harmonic in all of
B
. Now
the proof is the same as in
[
13
, Lemma 7.3]
, but we repeat it here anyway. First notice, that
u
is a classical harmonic function and in particular, fulfills the mean value property. We use it to deduce

\begin{split}Q\int_{B}|Du-Du(0)|^{2}&=Q\int_{B}\left(|Du|^{2}+|Du(0)|^{2}-2Du\cdot Du(0)\right)\\
&=Q\int_{B}|Du|^{2}+Q|B||Du(0)|^{2}-2Q\left(\int_{B}Du\right)\cdot Du(0)\\
&=Q\int_{B}|Du|^{2}-Q|B||Du(0)|^{2}.\end{split}

(14.3)

Similarly we compute

\begin{split}Q\int_{B}|Dw|^{2}&=\sum_{i}\int_{B}|Dw_{i}|^{2}=\sum_{i}\int_{B}\left(|Dw_{i}-Du(0)|^{2}-|Du(0)|^{2}+2Dw_{i}\cdot Du(0)\right)\\
&=\int_{B}{\mathcal{G}}(Dw,Q\left\llbracket{Du(0)}\right\rrbracket)^{2}-Q|B||Du(0)|^{2}+2Q\left(\int_{B}\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{i}Dw_{i}\right)\cdot Du(0)\\
&=\int_{B}{\mathcal{G}}(Dw,Q\left\llbracket{Du(0)}\right\rrbracket)^{2}+Q|B||Du(0)|^{2}.\end{split}

(14.4)

Last we split the Dirichlet energy of
w
into the average and the average-free part (as already observed in (
9.14
)).

\begin{split}\int_{B}|D\bar{w}|^{2}&=\sum_{i}\int_{B}|Dw_{i}-Du|^{2}=\sum_{i}\int_{B}\left(|Dw_{i}|^{2}+|Du|^{2}-2Dw_{i}\cdot Du\right)\\
&=\int_{B}|Dw|^{2}+Q\int_{B}|Du|^{2}-2Q\int_{B}\left(\frac{1}{Q}Dw_{i}\right)\cdot Du\\
&=\int_{B}|Dw|^{2}-Q\int_{B}|Du|^{2}.\end{split}

(14.5)

The three identities (
14.3
), (
14.4
), (
14.5
) and dividing everything by
2
conclude the lemma.
∎

An other important ingredient is the unique continuation for Dir-minimizers (compare to
[
13
, Lemma 7.1]
).

Lemma 14.6

(Unique Continuation for Dir-minimizers)
.

For every
0<\eta<1
and
c>0
, there is a
\delta>0
such that whenever
B_{2r}^{+}\subset V_{0}
is the half ball and
w:B_{2r}^{+}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
is Dir-minimizing with
w=Q\left\llbracket{0}\right\rrbracket
on
B_{2r}^{+}\cap({\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\})
,
\textup{Dir}(w,B^{+}_{2r})=1
, and
\textup{Dir}(w,B_{r}^{+})\geq c
, then

\textup{Dir}(w,B_{s}(q))\geq\delta\qquad\textnormal{ for every }B_{s}(q)\subset B^{+}_{2r}\textnormal{ with }s\geq\eta r.

Proof.

The qualitative statement (UC) of the proof of
[
13
, Lemma 7.1]
applies directly to our situation while the quantitative statement follows from a blow-up argument that goes analogously for us as
B_{s}(q)\subset B^{+}_{2r}
.
∎

The previous two lemmas imply the following energy decay for Dir-minimizers (compare to
[
13
, Proposition 7.2]
) which itself implies the Proposition
14.4
. First fix a number
\lambda>0
such that

(1+\lambda)^{4}<2^{\delta_{1}}.

Proposition 14.7

(Decay estimate for Dir-minimizers)
.

For any
\eta>0
there is a
\delta>0
such that whenever
B_{2r}^{+}\subset V_{0}
is the half ball and
w:B_{2r}^{+}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
is Dir-minimizing with
w=Q\left\llbracket{0}\right\rrbracket
on
B_{2r}^{+}\cap({\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\})
and satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{B^{+}_{(1+\lambda)r}}{\mathcal{G}}(Dw,Q\left\llbracket{D({\bm{\eta}}\circ w)(0)}\right\rrbracket)^{2}\geq 2^{\delta_{1}-4}\textup{Dir}(w,B_{2r}^{+}),

then we have for any
B_{s}(q)\subset B_{2r}^{+}
with
s\geq\eta r

\displaystyle\delta\ \textup{Dir}\left(w,B^{+}_{(1+\lambda)r}\right)\leq\textup{Dir}\left(\bar{w},B^{+}_{(1+\lambda)r}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\delta r^{2}}\int_{B_{s}(q)}|\bar{w}|^{2}.

Here we used again the notation
\bar{w}:=w\oplus(-{\bm{\eta}}\circ w)=\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{w_{i}-{\bm{\eta}}\circ w}\right\rrbracket
.

Proof.

We follow word by word the proof of
[
13
, Proposition 7.2]
using Lemma
14.6
and Lemma
14.5
instead of
[
13
, Lemma 7.1]
and
[
13
, Lemma 7.3]
. We reach the contradicting inequality

\int_{B^{+}_{1+\lambda}}|Du-Du(0)|^{2}\geq 2^{\delta_{1}-4}\int_{B^{+}_{2}}|Du|^{2}

which is false as one can see by reflecting such that
u
stays harmonic and then using the classical decay for harmonic functions.
∎

15.
Frequency function and monotonicity

In this section we take a further crucial step towards the proof of Theorem
4.6
. We recall our key Assumption
4.5
and we add a further one on the smallness of the excess. Before doing that, we observe a corollary of the decay estimate in Theorem
4.7
.

Corollary 15.1
.

Let
T
and
\Gamma
be as in Assumption
2.4
and assume that
0\in\Gamma
is a flat point and that
Q\left\llbracket{V}\right\rrbracket
is the unique tangent cone to
T
at
0
. Then there is a geometric constant
\kappa>0
and constants
C
and
r_{0}>0
(depending on
\Gamma
and
T
) such that

{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{r})\leq Cr^{4\kappa}\qquad\forall r\leq r_{0}\,.

(15.1)

Thus, upon rescaling the current appropriately, if
0
is a flat point we can assume, without loss of generality, the following.

Assumption 15.2
.

Let
T
and
\Gamma
be as in Assumption
2.4
.

0\in\Gamma
is a flat point,
Q\left\llbracket{V}\right\rrbracket
is the unique tangent cone to
T
at
0
, we let
n
be as in (
4.1
) and assume that (
4.3
) holds.
In addition we assume to have fixed a choice of the parameters so that Theorem
10.16
and Theorem
10.21
hold and that

{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}\rho})+\mathbf{A}^{2}\rho^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{CM}\rho^{2\kappa}\,\qquad\forall\rho\leq 1\,.

(15.2)

Observe that, by (
15.2
), we conclude that both Theorem
10.16
and Theorem
10.21
can be applied to the current
T_{0,\rho}
whenever
\rho\leq 1
.

15.1.
Intervals of flattening

We start defining a decreasing set of radii
\{t_{1}>t_{2}>\ldots\}\subset(0,1]
, which at the moment can be both finite and infinite: in the first case one
t_{N}
will be equal to
0
, while in the second case all
t_{k}
’s are positive and
t_{k}\downarrow 0
.

t_{1}
is defined to be equal to
2
. We then let
\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}={\mathcal{M}}_{1}
be the center manifold and
\bar{N}_{1}=N_{1}
the corresponding normal approximation which results after we apply Theorem
10.16
and Theorem
10.21
to the current
T
. Moreover we let
{\mathscr{W}}^{(1)}
be the squares of the Whitney decomposition described in Definition
10.11
. We then distinguish two cases:

(Stop)

There is a square
H\in{\mathscr{W}}^{(1)}
such that

{\rm{dist}}(0,H)\leq 64\sqrt{2}\ell(H)\,.

(15.3)

(Go)

There is no such square.

Notice that every such square
H
satisfying (
15.3
) is a boundary square. In the first case we select an
H
as in (Stop) which has maximal sidelength and we define
\bar{t}_{2}:=66\sqrt{2}\ell(H)
and
t_{2}:=t_{1}\bar{t}_{2}=132\sqrt{2}\ell(H)
. Otherwise we define
t_{2}=0
. Observe that

\frac{t_{2}}{t_{1}}\leq 66\sqrt{2}2^{-N_{0}}

(15.4)

Before proceeding further, we record an important consequence of the Whitney decomposition:

Corollary 15.3
.

If (Stop) holds, then the square
H
of maximal sidelength that satisfies (
15.3
) must be an element of
{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
, i.e. it violates the excess condition

Proof.

Observe that if
H
is an (NN) square, then there is a neighboring square
H^{\prime}
of double sidelength which also belongs to
{\mathscr{W}}
and it is easy to see that the latter satisfies (
15.3
) too, violating the maximilaity of
H
. Note next that (
15.3
) implies that
H
is a boundary square, and as such it cannot belong to
{\mathscr{W}}^{h}
.
∎

In case
t_{2}>0
we then apply Theorem
10.16
and Theorem
10.21
to
T_{0,t_{2}}
and let
\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}
and
\bar{N}_{2}
be the corresponding objects. The pair
({\mathcal{M}}_{2},N_{2})
will be derived by scaling back the objects at scale
t_{2}
, namely

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\{t_{2}q:q\in\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}\right\},

(15.5)

\displaystyle N_{2}(q)

\displaystyle=t_{2}\bar{N}_{2}\left(\frac{q}{t_{2}}\right)\,.

(15.6)

We then apply the procedure above to
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
in place of
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and determine
\bar{t}_{3}
analogously, while we set
t_{3}:=t_{2}\bar{t}_{3}
.

We proceed inductively and define
\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{k},{\mathcal{M}}_{k},\bar{N}_{k},N_{k}
,
\bar{t}_{k}
, and
t_{k}:=t_{k-1}\bar{t}_{k}
: the procedure stops when one
t_{k}
equals
0
, otherwise goes indefinitely. Observe that for every
k
we have the estimate

\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}}\leq 66\sqrt{2}2^{-N_{0}}.

(15.7)

15.2.
Frequency function

Observe that the conclusion of Theorem
4.6
is equivalent to
T
coinciding with
Q\left\llbracket{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\right\rrbracket
for some
k
in a neighborhood of the origin. A simple corollary of the interior regularity is in fact the following

Corollary 15.4
.

If
N_{k}\equiv Q\left\llbracket{0}\right\rrbracket
on some nontrivial open subset of
{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
, then
T=Q\left\llbracket{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\right\rrbracket
in a neighborhood of
0
and in particular Theorem
4.6
holds.

We next consider a function
d
which is
C^{2}
in the punctured ball
{\mathbf{B}}_{1}(0)
, whose gradient
\nabla d
is tangent to
\Gamma
and such that (i)-(ii)-(iii) of Definition
5.6
hold. Likewise we fix the function
\phi:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
given by

\phi(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&\mbox{if $t\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]$},\\
(1-2t),&\mbox{if $t\in[\frac{1}{2},1]$},\\
0,&\mbox{if $t\geq 1$}\,.\end{array}\right.

From now on we denote by
D
the classical Euclidean differentiation of functions, tensors, and vector fields, which for objects defined on the manifold
{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
will mean that we compute derivatives along the tangents to the manifold. On the other hand we use the notation
\nabla_{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}
,
D^{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}
, and
{\rm div}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}
, respectively for the gradient, Levi-Civita connection, and divergence of (respectively), functions, tensors, and vector fields on
{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
understood as a Riemannian submanifold of the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{2+n}
.

We then define

\displaystyle D(r)

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\phi\left(\frac{d(x)}{r}\right)|DN_{k}|^{2}(x)\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{2}(x),\qquad\mbox{if $r\in(t_{k+1},t_{k}]$},

(15.8)

\displaystyle H(r)

\displaystyle:=-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\phi^{\prime}\left(\frac{d(x)}{r}\right)|\nabla_{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}d(x)|^{2}\frac{|N_{k}(x)|^{2}}{d(x)}\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{2}(x),\qquad\mbox{if $r\in(t_{k+1},t_{k}]$}.

(15.9)

\displaystyle S(r)

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\phi\left(\frac{d(x)}{r}\right)|N_{k}(x)|^{2}\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{2}(x).

(15.10)

We are then ready to state our main estimate.

Theorem 15.5
.

Let
T
be as in Assumption
15.2
. Either
T=Q\left\llbracket{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\right\rrbracket
in a neighborhood of the origin for some
k
(and in that case note that
t_{k+1}=0
), or else
H(r)>0
and
D(r)>0
for every
r
. In the latter case the function
I(r):=\frac{rD(r)}{H(r)}
satisfies the following properties for some constants
C
and
\tau>0
:

(a)

For all
r>0
, we have

\displaystyle I(r)

\displaystyle\geq C^{-1},\,

(15.11)

and

D(r)\leq Cr^{2+\tau}\,.

(15.12)

(b)

I
is continuous and differentiable on each open interval
(t_{k+1},t_{k})
and moreover

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dr}\left(\log I(r)+CD(r)^{\tau}-Ct_{k}^{2\tau-2}\frac{S(r)}{D(r)}\right)

\displaystyle\geq-Cr^{\tau-1}\qquad\mbox{for a.e. $r\in]t_{k+1},t_{k}[$.}

(15.13)

(c)

At each
t_{k}
the function
I
has one-sided limits

\displaystyle I(t_{k}^{+})

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\downarrow t_{k}}I(t),

\displaystyle I(t_{k}^{-})

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\uparrow t_{k}}I(t),\,

and moreover

\displaystyle\sum_{k}|I(t_{k}^{+})-I(t_{k}^{-})|<\infty.

(15.14)

We will prove (a) and (b) in Section
16
while we devote Section
17
to show (c). An obvious corollary of Theorem
15.5
is the following

Corollary 15.6
.

Let
T
be as in Assumption
15.2
. Either
0
is a regular point, or else
I(r)
is well defined for every
r
and the limit

I_{0}:=\lim_{r\downarrow 0}I(r)

exists, is finite and positive.

Proof.

First of all observe that, since
I(r)\geq C^{-1}
,

f(r):=\log I(r)-Ct_{k}^{2\tau-2}\frac{S(r)}{D(r)}+CD(r)^{\tau}+Cr^{\tau}\geq-\log C\,.

We will also see below in Lemma
16.1
that
S(r)\leq Cr^{2}D(r)
. Hence, since the Lipschitz constant of
\log
is bounded on
[C^{-1},\infty[
, we infer

|f(t_{j}^{+})-f(t_{j}^{-})|\leq C|I(t_{j}^{+})-I(t_{j}^{-})|+C(t_{j}^{+})^{\tau}.

(15.15)

Next we show that the two bounds (
15.14
) and (
15.13
) imply that
f
is bounded from above: considering
\rho\in]0,1[
, we let
k
the largest number such that
\rho<t_{k}
and we can estimate

\displaystyle f(1)-f(\rho)

\displaystyle=\int_{\rho}^{t_{k}}f^{\prime}+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\int_{t_{j+1}}^{t_{j}}f^{\prime}+\sum_{j=2}^{k}(f(t_{j}^{+})-f(t_{j}^{-}))

which turns into

\displaystyle f(\rho)

\displaystyle\leq f(1)-\int_{\rho}^{t_{k}}f^{\prime}-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\int_{t_{j+1}}^{t_{j}}f^{\prime}-\sum_{j}|f(t_{j}^{+})-f(t_{j}^{-})|

\displaystyle\leq f(1)+C\int_{0}^{1}r^{\tau-1}\,dr+C\sum_{j}|I(t_{j}^{+})-I(t_{j}^{-})|<\infty\,

(note that in the last line we have used (
15.15
)).

Next observe that the distributional derivative of
f
consists of a nonnegative measure (on the union of the open intervals
(t_{k+1},t_{k})
and a purely atomic Radon measure which has finite mass by (
15.14
). We thus conclude that the distributional derivative of
f
is a Radon measure. Next fix any
\rho\leq 1
and let
t_{k}
be such that
2t_{k+1}<\rho<2t_{k}
. We then have the bound

\displaystyle|Df|(]\rho,1[)

\displaystyle\leq Df(\rho,t_{k}^{-})+\sum_{1\leq j\leq k-1}Df(]t_{j+1}^{+},t_{j}^{-}[)+\sum_{2\leq j\leq k}|f(t_{j}^{+})-f(t_{j}^{-})|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|f(t_{j}^{+})-f(t_{j}^{-})|+\|f\|_{\infty}<\infty\,.

Hence, letting
\rho
go to
0
we discover that
|Df|(]0,1[)<\infty
, that is
f\in BV(]0,1[)
. This in turn implies that
f
is a function of bounded variation and hence that
\lim_{r\downarrow 0}f(r)
exists and is finite. Observe, moreover that by (
16.11
) we infer that

f(r)-\log(I(r))
converges to
0
as
r\downarrow 0
. We thus conclude that

\lim_{r\downarrow 0}e^{f(r)}=\lim_{r\downarrow 0}I(r)

exists, it is finite, and it is positive.
∎

16.
Proof of Theorem
15.5
: Part I

16.1.
Proof of (
15.11
)

The claim is simply equivalent to the existence of a constant
C
such that
H(r)\leq CrD(r)
. The latter is a consequence of a Poincaré-type inequality which uses the fact that
N_{k}
vanishes identically on the boundary curve
\Gamma
. The proof will be reduced to
[
8
, Proposition 9.4]
. However, in order to make the latter reduction, we employ a device which will be used in several subsequent computations. Having fixed a positive
r
different from any
t_{j}
we let
k
be such that

t_{k+1}<r<t_{k}
and we define the corresponding rescaled quantities
\bar{D}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r),\bar{H}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r),\bar{S}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)
, and
\bar{I}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)
. More precisely we define the function
d_{k}(x):=t_{k}^{-1}d(t_{k}x)
and set

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{k}(\rho)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\phi\left(\frac{d_{k}(x)}{\rho}\right)|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}(x)\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{2}(x)\,,

(16.1)

\displaystyle\bar{H}_{k}(\rho)

\displaystyle:=-\int_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\phi^{\prime}\left(\frac{d_{k}(x)}{\rho}\right)|\nabla_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}d_{k}(x)|^{2}\frac{|\bar{N}_{k}(x)|^{2}}{d_{k}(x)}\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{2}(x)\,,

(16.2)

\displaystyle\bar{S}_{k}(\rho)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\phi\left(\frac{d_{k}(x)}{\rho}\right)|\bar{N}_{k}(x)|^{2}\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{2}(x).

(16.3)

We then can immediately check the relations

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)

\displaystyle=t_{k}^{-2}D(r)\,,

(16.4)

\displaystyle\bar{H}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)

\displaystyle=t_{k}^{-3}H(r)\,,

(16.5)

\displaystyle\bar{S}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)

\displaystyle=t_{k}^{-4}S(r)\,,

(16.6)

\displaystyle\bar{S}_{k}^{\prime}(t_{k}^{-1}r)

\displaystyle=t_{k}^{-3}S^{\prime}(r)\,,

(16.7)

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{k}^{\prime}(t_{k}^{-1}r)

\displaystyle=t_{k}^{-1}D^{\prime}(r)\,.

(16.8)

Lemma 16.1
.

There is a constant
C
such that

\displaystyle H(r)

\displaystyle\leq CrD(r)\,,

(16.9)

\displaystyle S^{\prime}(r)

\displaystyle\leq CrD(r)\,,

(16.10)

\displaystyle S(r)

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{2}D(r)\,.

(16.11)

Proof.

We observe that the corresponding inequalities for
\bar{D}_{k}
,
\bar{H}_{k}
,
\bar{S}_{k}
, and
\bar{S}_{k}^{\prime}

follow from
[
8
, Proposition 9.4]
, since the center manifold
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
, the functions
d_{k}
, and
N_{k}
satisfy the assumptions of the Proposition.
∎

16.2.
Derivatives of
H
and
D

In order to prove (
15.13
) the first step consists in computing the derivatives of
H
and
D
. In what follows we will use the usual convention of denoting by
O(g)
any function
f
of the real variable
r>0
with the property that
|f(r)|\leq Cg(r)
. Moreover, in order to avoid cumbersome notation, for
r\in(t_{k+1},t_{k}]
we will drop the subscript
\mathcal{M}_{k}
from the gradient
\nabla_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}
on the manifold.

Proposition 16.2
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
15.5
we have, for every
r\in(t_{k+1},t_{k}]
,

\displaystyle D^{\prime}(r)

\displaystyle=-\int\phi^{\prime}\left(\frac{d(x)}{r}\right)\frac{d(x)}{r^{2}}|DN|^{2},

(16.12)

\displaystyle H^{\prime}(r)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{r}+O(1)\right)H(r)+2E(r),

(16.13)

and

E(r)=-\frac{1}{r}\int\phi^{\prime}\left(\frac{d(x)}{r}\right)\sum_{i}N_{i}(x)\cdot(DN_{i}(x)\nabla d(x))\,.

(16.14)

Proof.

The first derivative is a straightforward computation. For the second, we can follow the computations of
[
8
, Proof of Proposition 9.5]
to conclude that

H^{\prime}(r)=2E(r)-\frac{1}{r}\int\phi^{\prime}\left(\frac{d(x)}{r}\right)\Delta_{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}d(x)|N|^{2}(x)\,,

where
\Delta_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator on the manifold
\mathcal{M}_{k}
. Noticing that
\phi^{\prime}\left(\frac{d(x)}{r}\right)
vanishes unless
C^{-1}r\leq|x|\leq Cr
, our claim will follow once we show that

\Delta_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}d(x)=\frac{1}{d(x)}+O(1)=\frac{1}{d(x)}(1+O(d(x)))\,.

In order to show the latter estimate, we fix a point
x\in\mathcal{M}_{k}
and observe first that the second fundamental form of the center manifold
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
is bounded by
C({\mathbf{E}}(T_{0,t_{k}},4R_{0})^{1/2}+\mathbf{A}t_{k})
, which in turn is bounded by
Ct_{k}^{\kappa}
for some positive
\kappa
. By rescaling, the second fundamental form
A_{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}
of
\mathcal{M}_{k}
enjoys the bound
\|A_{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\|_{\infty}\leq Ct_{k}^{\kappa-1}
. On the other hand, recalling that
|x|^{-1}|Dd-D|x||+|D^{2}d-D^{2}|x||\leq C
it is easy to see that

\displaystyle\left|\Delta_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}d(x)-\frac{1}{d(x)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\Delta_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}|x|-\frac{1}{|x|}\right|+C+C|x|\|A_{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C+C\|A_{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\|_{\infty}\leq Ct_{k}^{\kappa}+C\leq C\,.\qed

16.3.
First variations and approximate identities

We start by recalling that, since
T_{0,t_{k}}
is area-minimizing and
\partial T_{0,2t_{k}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}}=Q\left\llbracket{\Gamma_{k}}\right\rrbracket\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}}
, then
\delta T_{0,t_{k}}(X)=0
for every
X
which is tangent to
\Gamma
. In what follows we fix a
C^{3}
extension
\tilde{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}
of the function
\bar{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}
to
[-4,4]^{2}\subset V
(by increasing the
C^{3,\omega}
estimate on
{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}
by a constant factor) whose graph is the center manifold
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
and we denote by
\mathbf{p}_{k}
the orthogonal projection onto the graph of
\tilde{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}
(which is of course defined only in a suitable normal neighborhood of it). We then fix the two relevant vector fields with which we will test the stationarity condition:

\displaystyle X_{o}(p)

\displaystyle:=\phi\left(\frac{d_{k}(\mathbf{p}_{k}(p))}{r}\right)(p-\mathbf{p}_{k}(p)),

\displaystyle X_{i}(p)

\displaystyle:=-Y({\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)):=-\frac{1}{2}\phi\left(\frac{d_{k}(\mathbf{p}_{k}(p)))}{r}\right)\frac{\nabla d_{k}^{2}}{|\nabla d_{k}|^{2}}(\mathbf{p}_{k}(p))

(note that
\nabla
means the gradient
\nabla_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}
here).

Note that
X_{i}
is tangent to both
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
and
\Gamma_{k}
. Moreover, in
[
8
, Sections 9.4 and 9.5]
, the estimates are done separately on both sides of
\Gamma_{k}
. Thus, it applies to our situation directly with
{\mathcal{M}}^{+}=\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
. Note also that the fifth error terms vanish for us as our ”ambient manifold” is
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+2}
. We summarize the statements here
and first define the following function

\varphi_{k}(p):=\phi\left(\frac{d_{k}({\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p))}{r}\right).

We also introduce the rescaled quantity

\bar{E}_{k}(\rho):=-\frac{1}{\rho}\int_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\phi^{\prime}\left(\frac{d_{k}(x)}{\rho}\right)\sum_{i}(\bar{N}_{k})_{i}(x)\cdot(D(\bar{N}_{k})_{i}(x)\nabla d_{k}(x))

and record the corresponding relation with
E
, namely

\bar{E}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)=t_{k}^{-2}E(r)\,.

(16.15)

Proposition 16.3

(Outer variations)
.

Let
A_{k}
and
H_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}
denote the second fundamental form and the man curvature of
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
respectively. Assume
\frac{t_{k+1}}{t_{k}}<r<1
. Then we have

\displaystyle|\bar{D}_{k}(r)-\bar{E}_{k}(r)|=\left|\int_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\left(\varphi_{k}|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}+\sum_{i}((\bar{N}_{k})_{i}\otimes D\varphi_{k}):D(\bar{N}_{k})_{i}\right)\right|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{4}|\textnormal{Err}_{j}^{o}|,

(16.16)

with

\displaystyle\textnormal{Err}_{1}^{o}

\displaystyle:=-Q\int_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\varphi\langle H_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}},{\bm{\eta}}\circ\bar{N}_{k}\rangle,

\displaystyle|\textnormal{Err}_{2}^{o}|

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}|\varphi_{k}||A_{k}|^{2}|\bar{N}_{k}|^{2},

\displaystyle|\textnormal{Err}_{3}^{o}|

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathcal{M}}\left(|\varphi_{k}|(|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}|\bar{N}_{k}||A_{k}|+|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{4})+|D\varphi_{k}|(|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{3}|\bar{N}_{k}|+|D\bar{N}_{k}||\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}|A_{k}|)\right),

\displaystyle\textnormal{Err}_{4}^{o}

\displaystyle:=\delta\mathbf{T}_{\bar{F}_{k}}(X_{o})-\delta T_{0,t_{k}}(X_{o})=\delta\mathbf{T}_{\bar{F}_{k}}(X_{o}).

For the inner variation, we introduce first a bit more of notation. First of all, we see
D(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}
as a map from
T\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+2}
. Denoting the components of
(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}
by
(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}=((\bar{N}_{k})_{j}^{1},\dots,(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}^{n+2})
and choosing a vector field
Z
tangent to
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
, we write

D(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}(Z)=(D_{Z}(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}^{1},\dots,D_{Z}(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}^{n+2}).

Similarly, we have

D(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}D^{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}Y(Z)=D(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}(D^{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}Y(Z))=(D_{D^{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}Y(Z)}(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}^{1},\dots,D_{D^{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}Y(Z)}(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}^{n+2}).

Thus, for the scalar product
D(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}:D(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}D^{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}Y
, we choose an orthonormal frame
e_{1},e_{2}
of
T\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
and express

D(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}:D(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}D^{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}Y=\sum_{\ell}\langle D_{e_{\ell}}(\bar{N}_{k})_{j},D_{D^{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}Y(e_{\ell})}(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}\rangle=\sum_{\ell,i}D_{e_{\ell}}(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}^{i}D_{D^{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}Y(e_{\ell})}(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}^{i}.

We further introduce the quantity

G(r):=-r^{-2}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\phi\left(\frac{d}{r}\right)\frac{d}{|\nabla d|^{2}}\sum_{j}|D(N_{k})_{j}\cdot\nabla d|^{2}

and its correspoding rescaled version

\bar{G}_{k}(\rho)=-\rho^{-2}\int_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\phi\left(\frac{d_{k}}{\rho}\right)\frac{d_{k}}{|\nabla d_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{j}|D(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}\cdot\nabla d_{k}|^{2}\,,

while we record the corresponding relation as in (
16.4
)-(
16.8
):

\bar{G}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)=t_{k}^{-1}G(r)\,.

(16.17)

Proposition 16.4

(Inner variations)
.

Under the above assumptions we have

\displaystyle\left|\bar{D}_{k}^{\prime}(r)-O(t_{k}^{\kappa})\bar{D}_{k}(r)-2\bar{G}_{k}(r)\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{r}\left|\int_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\left(\sum_{j}D(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}:D(\bar{N}_{k})_{j}D^{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}Y-\frac{1}{2}|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}{\rm div}_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}Y\right)\right|\leq\frac{2}{r}\sum_{j=1}^{4}|\textnormal{Err}_{j}^{i}|,

(16.18)

with

\displaystyle\textnormal{Err}_{1}^{i}

\displaystyle:=Q\int_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\left(\langle H_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}},{\bm{\eta}}\circ\bar{N}_{k}\rangle{\text{div}}_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}Y+\langle D_{Y}H_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}},{\bm{\eta}}\circ\bar{N}_{k}\rangle\right),

\displaystyle|\textnormal{Err}_{2}^{i}|

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}|A_{k}|^{2}\left(|DY||\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}+|Y||\bar{N}_{k}||D\bar{N}_{k}|\right),

\displaystyle|\textnormal{Err}_{3}^{i}|

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}\left(|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}|Y||A_{k}|(|\bar{N}_{k}|+|D\bar{N}_{k}|)+|DY|(|A||\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}|D\bar{N}_{k}|+|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{4})\right)

\displaystyle\textnormal{Err}_{4}^{i}

\displaystyle:=\delta\mathbf{T}_{\bar{F}_{k}}(X_{i})-\delta T_{0,t_{k}}(X_{i})=\delta\mathbf{T}_{\bar{F}_{k}}(X_{i}).

Proof.

The arguments for the proposition are the same as in
[
8
, Proposition 9.10]
and indeed they are based on the Taylor expansions of
[
12
, Theorems 4.2 & 4.3]
. However some more care is required because the term
O(t_{k}^{\kappa})D(r)
appears in the corresponding inequality (namely
[
8
, (9.28)]
as
O(1)D(r)
. The reason for the improvement is based on the computations

[
8
, (9.29)]
and
[
8
, Lemma 9.2]
: the improvement follows easily from the fact that:

•

The curvature of the rescaled boundary
\Gamma_{k}
is bounded by
t_{k}
;

•

The
C^{3}
norm of the function

\bar{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}
(whose graph is the center manifold
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
) is bounded by
({\mathbf{E}}(T_{0,t_{k}},{\mathbf{C}}_{4R_{0}})+\|\psi_{k}\|_{C^{3,\alpha_{0}}})^{1/2}
,
where
\psi_{k}
is the function whose graph describes
\Gamma_{k}
; we thus have
\|\bar{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}\|_{C^{3}}\leq Ct_{k}^{\tau}
.

∎

16.4.
Families of subregions for estimating the error terms

We want to estimate the error terms over the Whitney regions in order to use the separation estimate (Proposition
14.1
) and the splitting before tilting estimates (Proposition
14.4
). To achieve this goal we goes along the same lines of
[
8
, Section 9.6]
and apply the arguments of
[
8
, Section 9.6]
to the current
T_{0,t_{k}}
that gives rise to the center manifold
\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{k}
. Notice that in each error term, there is the cut-off
\phi(d_{k}/r)
, thus it is enough to consider squares which intersect
\mathscr{B}^{+}_{r}:=\{x\in V_{0}\cap D:d_{k}(\bar{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}(x))<r\}
. However, to sum the estimates over all squares, we prefer the regions over which we integrate to be disjoint. For this purpose, we define a Besicovitch-type covering.

From now on we fix all the constants from Assumption
10.7
and treat them as geometric constants. We are going to consider the Whitney decomposition and the corresponding family
{\mathscr{W}}^{e},{\mathscr{W}}^{h},{\mathscr{W}}^{n}
of squares whose definition is detailed in Section
10
. Note that the construction is not applied to the current
T
and the boundary
\Gamma
, but rather to the rescaled current
T_{0,t_{k}}
and the rescaled boundary
\Gamma_{k}
. Note that the assumptions for the construction apply for each
k
. For our notation to be more precise we should add the dependence on
k
of the various families
{\mathscr{W}}
, however, since
k
is fixed at this stage, in order to make our formulas simpler we drop such dependence.

First we consider all squares which stopped for the excess or the height and which influence some square intersecting
\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}
.

Definition 16.5
.

We define the family
{\mathcal{T}}
to be

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}\cup{\mathscr{W}}^{h}:L\cap\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}\neq\emptyset\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cup\left\{L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}:\textnormal{ there is an }L^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{W}}^{n}(L)\textnormal{ such that }L^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}\neq\emptyset\right\}.

Notice that because in a chain of squares in
{\mathscr{W}}^{n}
, the sidelengths always double, we have for each
L\in{\mathcal{T}}

\textnormal{sep}(L,\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}):=\inf\{|x-y|:x\in L,y\in\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}\}\leq 3\sqrt{2}\ell(L).

To each such square
L\in{\mathcal{T}}
, we associate a ball
B(L)
which we call
satellite ball
. Preferably this ball is contained in the square and with radius comparable to the sidelength. However, as not every square in
{\mathcal{T}}
is contained in
D
, we choose instead a nearby ball. Moreover we want that the concentric ball with twice the radius to be contained in
\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}
. Notice that because of the intervals of flattening (
15.3
), the largest square
L
contributing to the center manifold and intersecting
\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}
satisfies
\ell(L)\leq\frac{1}{64\sqrt{2}}r
.

•

If
B_{\ell(L)/2}(x_{L})\subset\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}
, we define
B(L):=B_{\ell(L)/4}(x_{L})
.

•

If
B_{\ell(L)/2}(x_{L})\nsubseteq\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}
, we choose a point
y\in\partial\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}
minimizing the distance to
L
. Notice that the size length of the squares in the domain of influence of
L
vary by a factor
2
, we have
|x_{L}-y|\leq 4\sqrt{2}\ell(L)
.
The center of the satellite ball we want to be a point inside
\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}
and close to
y
(and thus close to
x_{L}
). Indeed, first notice that by the regularity assumption on
\Gamma_{k}
,
\bar{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}
(Theorem
10.16
) and
d_{k}
(Definition
5.6
) there is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\Psi_{r}:\bar{B}_{r}^{+}\to\bar{\mathscr{B}}_{r}^{+}
with
\|\Psi_{r}-\text{Id}\|\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
. Moreover, we define for any
\ell<\frac{r}{2}
the vectorfield
n_{\ell}:\partial B_{r}^{+}\to B_{r}^{+}
describing
\partial\{y\in B_{r}^{+}:{\rm{dist}}(y,\partial B_{r}^{+})>\ell\}
by

\displaystyle n_{\ell}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\begin{cases}(x_{1},\ell),&\textnormal{if }|x_{1}|<r-\ell,\ x_{2}=0\,,\\
(r-\ell)(x_{1},x_{2}),&\textnormal{if }x_{2}>\ell\,,\\
(r-\ell,\ell),&\textnormal{if }\ell-r<x_{1}<r,\ x_{2}\leq\ell\,,\\
(-r+\ell,\ell),&\textnormal{if }-r<x_{1}<-r+\ell,\ x_{2}\leq\ell.\end{cases}

Notice that if
{\varepsilon}_{CM}
is small enough, we have for any
\ell<\frac{r}{2}

B_{\ell/2}\big(\Psi_{r}(n_{\ell}(x))\big)\subset\Psi_{r}\big(B_{\ell}(n_{\ell}(x))\big)\subset\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}.

Thus for the
y\in B_{\ell(L)/2}(x_{L})\cap\partial\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}
, we define

q_{L}:=\Psi_{r}\big(n_{\ell(L)/2}(\Psi_{r}^{-1}(y))\big)

and observe that

B(L):=B_{\ell(L)/4}(q_{L})\subset\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}\,.

By construction and the estimates on
d_{k}
, we have if
{\varepsilon}_{CM}
is small enough,

\displaystyle|q_{L}-x_{L}|\leq 5\sqrt{2}\ell(L)\qquad\textnormal{ and thus }\qquad{\rm{dist}}(q_{L},L)\leq 4\sqrt{2}\ell(L).

From this family
{\mathcal{T}}
, we now choose a maximal subfamily
\mathscr{T}
for which the satellite balls are disjoint. Denote by
S:=\sup\{\ell(L):L\in{\mathcal{T}}\}
. We define
\mathscr{T}_{1}\subset\{L\in{\mathcal{T}}:\frac{1}{2}S\leq\ell(L)\leq S\}
to be a maximal subfamily for which the associated satellite balls are pairwise disjoint. We inductively define
\mathscr{T}_{k+1}\subset\{L\in{\mathcal{T}}:2^{-k-1}S\leq\ell(L)\leq 2^{-k}S\}
to be a maximal subfamily such that all the satellite balls
B(L^{\prime})
with
L^{\prime}\in\mathscr{T}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{T}_{k}
are pairwise disjoint. Finally we define
\mathscr{T}
to be the union of all the
\mathscr{T}_{k}
. As we want to cover all of
\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}
, we associate to each square in
L\in\mathscr{T}
the nearby squares of
{\mathcal{T}}
whose satellite balls intersect
B(L)
and the domain of influence
{\mathscr{W}}^{n}(L)
. Indeed, by a standard covering argument, notice that if
H\in{\mathcal{T}}
, then there is at least one square
L\in\mathscr{T}
such that
{\rm{dist}}(H,L)\leq 20\sqrt{2}\ell(L)
. We fix an arbitrary choice to partition
{\mathcal{T}}
into families
{\mathcal{T}}(L)
such that
L\in\mathscr{T}
, for any
H\in{\mathcal{T}}(L)
we have
\ell(H)\leq 2\ell(L)
and
{\rm{dist}}(H,L)\leq 20\sqrt{2}\ell(L)
. Now we add the rest of
\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}
and define

{\mathscr{W}}(L):=\bigcup_{H\in{\mathcal{T}}(L)}{\mathscr{W}}^{n}(H)\cup\{H\}.

The associated Whitney regions will be called
{\mathcal{U}}(L)\subset{\mathcal{M}}
,

{\mathcal{U}}(L):=\bigcup_{H\in{\mathscr{W}}(L)}\bar{\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(H)\,,

where the map
\bar{\bm{\Phi}}_{k}
is the parametrization of the center manifold induced by
\bar{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}
, namely
\bar{\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x)=(x,\bar{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}(x))
.

For simplicity of notation, we enumerate
\mathscr{T}=\{L_{i}\}_{i}
and denote

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{r}^{+}

\displaystyle:=\bar{\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+})=\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}\cap\{d_{k}<r\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{i}

\displaystyle:={\mathcal{U}}(L_{i})\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{r}^{+},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}^{i}

\displaystyle:=\bar{\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(B(L_{i})),

\displaystyle\ell_{i}

\displaystyle:=\ell(L_{i}).

Notice that by construction, every satellite ball
B(L_{i})
has distance at least
\ell_{i}/4
to
\partial\mathscr{B}_{r}^{+}
. In particular, there is a geometric constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle c\frac{\ell_{i}}{r}\leq\inf_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}^{i})}\varphi_{k}=\inf_{{\mathcal{B}}^{i}}\varphi_{k}.

As in
[
8
, Section 9.6.2]
, we conclude that there is a geometric constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k}^{-1}({\mathcal{U}}_{i})}\varphi_{k}=\sup_{{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}\varphi_{k}

\displaystyle\leq C\inf_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k}^{-1}({\mathcal{U}}_{i})}\varphi_{k}=C\inf_{{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}\varphi_{k},

(16.19)

\displaystyle\sum_{H\in{\mathscr{W}}(L_{i})}\ell(H)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\ell_{i}^{2}.

(16.20)

Applying the estimates of Theorem
10.21
and Corollary
10.19
(ii) in each square of
{\mathscr{W}}(L_{i})
and summing over them yields

\displaystyle{\rm{Lip}}(\bar{N}_{k}|_{{\mathcal{U}}_{i}})

\displaystyle\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\gamma_{2}}\ell_{i}^{\gamma_{2}}\,,

(16.21)

\displaystyle\|\bar{N}_{k}\|_{C^{0}({\mathcal{U}}_{i})}+\sup_{{\rm spt}(T)\cap{\mathbf{p}}^{-1}({\mathcal{U}}_{i})}|{\mathbf{p}}^{\perp}|

\displaystyle\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\ell_{i}^{1+\beta_{1}}\,,

(16.22)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{T}_{\bar{F}_{k}}-T_{0,t_{k}}\|({\mathbf{p}}_{k}^{-1}({\mathcal{U}}_{i}))

\displaystyle\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}^{1+\gamma_{2}}\ell_{i}^{4+\gamma_{2}}\,,

(16.23)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}\,\ell_{i}^{4-2\delta_{1}}\,,

(16.24)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}|{\bm{\eta}}\circ\bar{N}_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell_{i}^{4+\nicefrac{{\gamma_{2}}}{{2}}}+C\int_{{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}|\bar{N}_{k}|^{2+\gamma_{2}}\,.

(16.25)

On the other hand, we can use the the Separation Proposition
14.1
, the Splitting Proposition
14.4
and the estimates (
16.19
), (
16.20
) to deduce estimates on the normal approximation as stated in the next lemma.

Lemma 16.6
.

Assume the assumption
10.18
holds. Then there is a geometric constant
C_{0}

2
2

2

Here and in the sequel we call a constant geometric if it depends only on
n,Q,N_{0},M_{0},C_{e}^{\flat},C_{e}^{\natural},C_{h}
which we fixed.
such that

\displaystyle{\bm{m}}_{0}\sum_{i}\left(\ell_{i}^{4+2\beta_{1}}~\inf_{{\mathcal{B}}^{i}}\varphi_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\bar{D}_{k}(r),

(16.26)

\displaystyle{\bm{m}}_{0}\sum_{i}\ell_{i}^{4+\beta_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\int_{{\mathcal{B}}_{r}^{+}}|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}\leq C_{0}(\bar{D}_{k}(r)+r\bar{D}_{k}^{\prime}(r)).

(16.27)

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle{\bm{m}}_{0}\sup_{i}\ell_{i}\leq C_{0}(r\bar{D}_{k}(r))^{1/(5+\beta_{1})}\quad\textnormal{ and }\quad{\bm{m}}_{0}\sup_{i}\left(\ell_{i}\inf_{{\mathcal{B}}^{i}}\varphi_{k}\right)\leq C_{0}\bar{D}_{k}(r)^{1/(4+\beta_{1})},

(16.28)

and

\bar{D}_{k}(r)\leq C_{0}{\bm{m}}_{0}r^{4-2\delta_{1}}\leq C_{0}t_{k}^{2\kappa}r^{4-2\delta_{1}}\,.

(16.29)

Proof.

The proof goes completely analogous to the one of
[
8
, Lemma 9.13]
and we summarize it here. Fix an
L_{i}\in\mathscr{T}
. If
L_{i}\in{\mathscr{W}}^{h}
, it is an interior square and we can use Proposition
14.1
to deduce

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{i}}|\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}\geq c_{0}{\bm{m}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\ell^{4+2\beta_{1}}_{i}.

(16.30)

On the other hand, if
L_{i}\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
, then
L_{i}
can be either a boundary square or an interior square. However the satellite ball does not intersect the boundary and also we can apply Proposition
14.4
in both situations. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{i}}|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq c_{0}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell_{i}^{4-2\delta_{1}},

(16.31)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{i}}\varphi|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq c_{0}{\bm{m}}_{0}\ell_{i}^{4-2\delta_{1}}~\inf_{{\mathcal{B}}^{i}}\varphi_{k}.

(16.32)

Summing over all squares and using (
16.30
), (
16.31
) and (
16.32
), we conclude

\displaystyle{\bm{m}}_{0}\sum_{i}\ell_{i}^{4+2\beta_{1}}~\inf_{{\mathcal{B}}^{i}}\varphi_{k}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{+}_{r}}\left(|\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}+\varphi_{k}|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}\right),

\displaystyle{\bm{m}}_{0}\sum_{i}\ell_{i}^{4+2\beta_{1}}\leq C_{0}\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{+}_{r}}

\displaystyle\left(|\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}+|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}\right)\leq C_{0}\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{+}_{r}}|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2},

where we used the Poincaré inequality and the fact that
\bar{N}_{k}
vanishes on
\Gamma_{k}
.
We conclude by noticing that, as
\phi^{\prime}=-2
in
[\frac{1}{2},1]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\{r/2<d_{k}<r\}\cap\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq r\bar{D}_{k}^{\prime}(r)\,,

\displaystyle\int_{\{d_{k}<r/2\}\cap\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}|D\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{D}_{k}(r)\,.

(
16.29
) is a consequence of (
16.24
).
∎

We end this section with estimating the error terms (compare with
[
8
, Proposition 9.14]
).

Proposition 16.7
.

There are constants
C,\tau>0
such that

\displaystyle|\textnormal{Err}_{1}^{o}|+|\textnormal{Err}_{3}^{o}|+|\textnormal{Err}_{4}^{o}|

\displaystyle\leq C\bar{D}_{k}(r)^{1+\tau},

(16.33)

\displaystyle|\textnormal{Err}_{2}^{o}|

\displaystyle\leq Ct_{k}^{2\kappa}\bar{S}_{k}(r)\leq Ct_{k}^{2\kappa}r^{2}\bar{D}_{k}(r)

(16.34)

and

\displaystyle|\textnormal{Err}_{1}^{i}|+|\textnormal{Err}_{3}^{i}|+|\textnormal{Err}_{4}^{i}|

\displaystyle\leq C\bar{D}_{k}(r)^{\tau}\big(\bar{D}_{k}(r)+r\bar{D}_{k}^{\prime}(r)\big),

(16.35)

\displaystyle|\textnormal{Err}_{2}^{i}|

\displaystyle\leq Ct_{k}^{2\kappa}r\bar{D}_{k}(r).

(16.36)

Proof.

The detailed estimates can be found in the proof of
[
8
, Proposition 9.14]
. Notice that as there it is done for either side of the boundary separately, and as we have the same estimates on
N
, it applies directly to our situation. The idea is as follows. First we notice that

|Y(p)|\leq\varphi(p)d_{k}({\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p))\quad\textnormal{ and }\quad|DY(p)|\leq C\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{B}}_{r}^{+}}({\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)).

Then because of the Theorem
10.16
, both the second fundamental form and the mean curvature of
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
are bounded (and their derivatives) are bouned by
Ct_{k}^{\kappa}
. The remaining terms in the errors can be split into the regions
{\mathcal{U}}_{j}
and then be estimated by powers of
{\bm{m}}_{0}
and
\ell_{j}
using (
16.21
) - (
16.25
). Choosing
\tau\ll\delta_{1}
and recalling that
\delta_{1}\leq\beta_{1}\leq\gamma_{1}/8
, we see that the powers are higher than what we need for (
16.26
) and (
16.27
). Thus with (
16.28
) we gain the additional
\bar{D}_{k}(r)^{\tau}
.

The only relevant difference in the estimates of
[
8
, Proposition 9.14]
is in the terms
\textnormal{Err}_{2}^{i}
and
\textnormal{E}_{2}^{o}
, where our estimates have an improved factor
Ct_{k}^{2\kappa}
in the right hand side. But this follows easily from the fact that in our case we take advantage of
\|A_{k}\|_{\infty}\leq Ct_{k}^{\kappa}
, while in
[
8
, Proposition 9.14]
the second fundamental form of the center manifold is only known to be bounded by a constant.
∎

16.5.
Proof (
15.12
) and (
15.13
)

In order to prove (
15.12
) we exploit (
16.4
) and (
16.29
): we assume
t_{k+1}<r<t_{k}
and estimate

D(r)=t_{k}^{2}\bar{D}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)\leq Ct_{k}^{2+2\kappa}(t_{k}^{-1}r)^{4-2\delta_{1}}\leq Cr^{2+2\kappa}\,.

In order to prove (
15.13
) we follow the computations of
[
8
, Section 9.1]
, but in our setting some additional complications are created by the fact that we need to scale back our estimates for the rescaled quantities
\bar{D}_{k}
,
\bar{H}_{k}
,
\bar{S}_{k}
,
\bar{G}_{k}
, and
\bar{S}_{k}
. First of all we recall (
16.13
):

H^{\prime}(r)=r^{-1}H(r)+2E(r)+O(1)H(r)\,.

(16.37)

Next we combine (
16.16
), (
16.33
), and (
16.34
) to get

|\bar{D}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)-\bar{E}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)|\leq C\bar{D}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)^{1+\tau}+Ct_{k}^{2\tau}\bar{S}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r).

(16.38)

We next can use (
16.4
), (
16.6
), and (
16.15
) to conclude

|D(r)-E(r)|\leq CD(r)(t_{k}^{-2}D(r))^{\tau}+Ct_{k}^{2\tau-2}S(r)\,.

(16.39)

Next recall that
D(r)\leq Cr^{2+2\kappa}
. Since
r\leq t_{k}
we can write

t_{k}^{-2}D(r)\leq Ct_{k}^{-2}r^{2}D(r)^{1-\nicefrac{{2}}{{(2+2\kappa)}}}\leq CD(r)^{1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{(1+\kappa)}}}\,.

Thus, at the prize of choosing
\tau
smaller, we can translate (
16.39
) into

|D(r)-E(r)|\leq CD(r)^{1+\tau}+Ct_{k}^{2\tau-2}S(r)\,.

(16.40)

The final ingredient is derived by first combining (
16.18
), (
16.35
), and (
16.36
) to get

\displaystyle|\bar{D}_{k}^{\prime}(t_{k}^{-1}r)+O(t_{k}^{2\kappa})\bar{D}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)-\bar{G}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{t_{k}^{-1}r}\bar{D}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)^{\tau}\left(\bar{D}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)+t_{k}^{-1}r\bar{D}_{k}^{\prime}(t_{k}^{-1}r)\right)+Ct_{k}^{2\kappa}\bar{D}_{k}(t_{k}^{-1}r)\,,

(16.41)

which in turn, using (
16.4
), (
16.17
), and (
16.15
) becomes

|D^{\prime}(r)+O(t_{k}^{2\kappa-1})D(r)-2G(r)|\leq C(t_{k}^{-2}D(r))^{\tau}(r^{-1}D(r)+D^{\prime}(r))+Ct_{k}^{2\kappa-1}D(r)\,.

(16.42)

But then, arguing as for (
16.40
) we can achieve

|D^{\prime}(r)-2G(r)|\leq Ct_{k}^{2\kappa-1}D(r)+CD(r)^{\tau}(r^{-1}D(r)+D^{\prime}(r))\,.

(16.43)

We are now ready to estimate
\frac{d}{dr}\log I(r)
. We start by writing

\frac{d}{dr}\log I(r)=\frac{1}{r}+\frac{D^{\prime}(r)}{D(r)}-\frac{H^{\prime}(r)}{H(r)}\,.

(16.44)

Hence, using (
16.37
) we write

\frac{d}{dr}\log I(r)\geq-C+\frac{D^{\prime}(r)}{D(r)}-\frac{2E(r)}{H(r)}\,.

(16.45)

Next recall (
15.12
)
while Lemma
16.1
implies that for
\sigma\in]0,1[
we have

t_{k}^{2\sigma-2}S(r)\leq Cr^{2}t_{k}^{2\sigma-2}D(r)\leq Cr^{2\sigma}D(r)\,.

(16.46)

In combination with the last two bounds, (
16.40
) becomes (after possibly choosing a new positive
\tau
)

|D(r)-E(r)|\leq Cr^{\tau}D(r)\,,

(16.47)

which in turn implies

\frac{D(r)}{2}\leq E(r)\leq 2D(r)\,,

(16.48)

provided
r\leq r_{0}
is sufficiently small with
r_{0}>0
depending only on
C
and
\tau
.

By (
16.48
) we can turn (
16.40
) into

|E(r)^{-1}-D(r)^{-1}|\leq CD(r)^{\tau-1}+Ct_{k}^{2\tau-2}\frac{S(r)}{D(r)^{2}}\,.

(16.49)

Inserting the latter into (
16.45
) (and considering that
D^{\prime}(r)\geq 0
) we then get

\frac{d}{dr}\log(I(r))\geq\frac{D^{\prime}(r)}{E(r)}-\frac{2E(r)}{H(r)}-C\frac{D^{\prime}(r)}{D(r)^{1-\tau}}-Ct_{k}^{2\kappa-2}\frac{S(r)D^{\prime}(r)}{D(r)^{2}}-C\,.

(16.50)

We can finally insert (
16.43
) to achieve

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dr}\log(I(r))\geq

\displaystyle\frac{2G(r)}{E(r)}-\frac{2E(r)}{H(r)}-C\frac{D(r)}{E(r)}\left(\frac{D(r)^{\tau}}{r}+\frac{D^{\prime}(r)}{D(r)^{1-\tau}}+t_{k}^{2\tau-2}\right)

\displaystyle-C\frac{D^{\prime}(r)}{D(r)^{1-\tau}}-Ct_{k}^{2\kappa-2}\frac{S(r)D^{\prime}(r)}{D(r)^{2}}-C\,.

(16.51)

Next note that:

•

G(r)H(r)\geq E(r)^{2}
, by Cauchy-Schwarz;

•

\frac{D(r)}{E(r)}\leq C
;

•

D(r)\leq Cr^{2+2\kappa}
.

•

We can rewrite
-\frac{S(r)D^{\prime}(r)}{D(r)^{2}}=\frac{d}{dr}\frac{S(r)}{D(r)}-\frac{S^{\prime}(r)}{D(r)}
, and it is easy to see that
S^{\prime}
is positive.

So, after possibly choosing
\tau
smaller, yet positive, we achieve

\frac{d}{dr}\left(\log I(r)+CD(r)^{\tau}-Ct_{k}^{2\tau-2}\frac{S(r)}{D(r)}\right)\geq-Cr^{\tau-1}\,.

(16.52)

17.
Proof of Theorem
15.5
: Part II

This section is devoted to prove (
15.14
). We observe that, by the continuity of the functions

\displaystyle t\mapsto H(N_{k},t)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad t\mapsto D(N_{k},t)

we have

\displaystyle I(t_{k}^{+})=\frac{t_{k}D(N_{k-1},t_{k})}{H(N_{k-1},t_{k})}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad I(t_{k}^{-})=\frac{t_{k}D(N_{k},t_{k})}{H(N_{k},t_{k})}\,.

In order to simplify our notation we use the shortcut
{\mathbf{E}}(T,r)
for
{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{B}}_{r})
. We will show the following two propositions

Proposition 17.1
.

There is a constant
C
independent of
k
such that, if
\varepsilon_{CM}
is small enough then

\displaystyle C^{-1}t_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})

\displaystyle\leq D(N_{k-1},t_{k})\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})

(17.1)

\displaystyle C^{-1}t_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})

\displaystyle\leq D(N_{k},t_{k})\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})

(17.2)

\displaystyle C^{-1}t_{k}^{3}{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})

\displaystyle\leq H(N_{k-1},t_{k})\leq Ct_{k}^{3}{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})

(17.3)

\displaystyle C^{-1}t_{k}^{3}{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})

\displaystyle\leq H(N_{k},t_{k})\leq Ct_{k}^{3}{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})\,.

(17.4)

Proposition 17.2
.

There is a positive exponent
\tau_{1}
independent of
k
such that, if
\varepsilon_{CM}
is small enough then

\displaystyle|D(N_{k-1},t_{k})-

\displaystyle D(N_{k},t_{k})|\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})^{1+\tau_{1}},

(17.5)

\displaystyle|H(N_{k-1},t_{k})-

\displaystyle H(N_{k},t_{k})|\leq Ct_{k}^{3}{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})^{1+\tau_{1}}.

(17.6)

Observe that the estimates (
17.2
) (the second one), (
17.3
) (the first one), (
17.4
) (the first one), (
17.5
), and (
17.6
) imply

|I(t_{k}^{+})-I(t_{k}^{-})|\leq C{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})^{\tau_{1}}\leq Ct_{k}^{2\kappa\tau_{1}}\,.

(17.7)

On the other hand, by the choice of
N_{0}
in Assumption
10.7
, by (
15.7
), we get
\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
, which iterated implies
t_{k}\leq 2^{-k}
. We therefore get

|I(t_{k}^{+})-I(t_{k}^{-})|\leq C2^{-2\kappa\tau_{1}k}\,,

(17.8)

which clearly implies (
15.14
).

Proof of Proposition
17.1
.

As the center manifold
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}
stopped, and we are close to the boundary, it must have stopped for the excess and thus, there is a square
L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
such that
c\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}}\leq\ell(L)\leq C\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}}
(recall section
15.1
). Looking at its ancestors (as we did in Proposition
10.12
), we notice

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}(T,\rho t_{k})={\mathbf{E}}(T_{0,t_{k-1}},\rho t_{k}/t_{k-1})\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}(k-1)\left(\rho\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}}\right)^{2-2\delta_{1}}\,,

(17.9)

for every
1\leq\rho\leq 5R_{0}\frac{t_{k-1}}{t_{k}}
and some geometric constant
C
. Here we denote by
{\bm{m}}_{0}(k-1)
and
{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)
the two quantities

\displaystyle{\bm{m}}_{0}(k-1)

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}(T_{0,t_{k-1}},{\mathbf{C}}_{5R_{0}})+\|\psi_{k-1}\|^{2}_{C^{3,\alpha}(]-5R_{0},5R_{0}[)},

\displaystyle{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}(T_{0,t_{k}},{\mathbf{C}}_{5R_{0}})+\|\psi_{k}\|^{2}_{C^{3,\alpha}(]-5R_{0},5R_{0}[)}\,,

where
\psi_{k}
and
\psi_{k-1}
are the functions describing the rescaled boundaries
\Gamma_{k}
and
\Gamma_{k-1}
. Observe that, since
\psi_{k}(0)=\psi_{k-1}(0)=0
and
\psi^{\prime}_{k}(0)=\psi^{\prime}_{k-1}(0)=0
, it can be readily checked that

\|\psi_{k}\|^{2}_{C^{3,\alpha}(]-5R_{0},5R_{0}[)}\leq\frac{t_{k}^{2}}{t_{k-1}^{2}}\|\psi_{k-1}\|^{2}_{C^{3,\alpha}(]-5R_{0},5R_{0}[)}\,,

so that we have

\displaystyle{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)\leq{\mathbf{E}}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{5R_{0}t_{k}})+\frac{t_{k}^{2}}{t_{k-1}^{2}}{\bm{m}}_{0}(k-1)\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}(k-1)\left(\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}}\right)^{2-2\delta_{1}},

(17.10)

where we also used (
17.9
).
On the other hand, because of the stopping condition we also know that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})={\mathbf{E}}(T_{0,t_{k-1}},6t_{k}/t_{k-1})\geq C^{-1}{\bm{m}}_{0}(k-1)\left(\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}}\right)^{2-2\delta_{1}}.

(17.11)

In particular, we infer by (
17.10
) that

{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})\geq C^{-1}{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)\,.

(17.12)

Observe now that for
D(\bar{N}_{k},1)
we have the inequality

D(\bar{N}_{k},1)\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)

by construction of the center manifold (i.e. (
10.21
)). In turn, by rescaling, we can conclude

D(N_{k},t_{k})=t_{k}^{2}D(\bar{N}_{k},1)\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})\,,

namely the first of the two inequalities in (
17.13
).
Then we observe that (
17.1
) and (
17.3
) follow from the Splitting Proposition
14.4
applied to to the current
T_{0,t_{k}}
which in turn produces the center manifold
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}
and the normal approximation
\bar{N}_{k-1}
as we are in the situation where the center manifold stopped.
Moreover, we recall that by the Poincaré inequality (as already observed in (
15.11
) and proved in Section
16
), we have for any
r>0

H(N_{k},r)\leq CrD(N_{k},r)\,.

Thus (
17.4
) and (
17.2
) follow once we have shown the following inequalities

\displaystyle D(N_{k},t_{k})\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})\leq Ct_{k}^{-1}H(N_{k},6t_{k})\,.

(17.13)

For the second inequality in (
17.13
) we adapt the proof of
[
13
, Proposition 3.7]
as the only difference to our situation is the cut-off function. We describe here the idea of the argument, the details can be read in
[
13
, Section 9]
.
Again recall the square
L\in{\mathscr{W}}^{e}
which stopped in the construction of
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}
according to the argument above. By the splitting Proposition
14.4
, we then have a nearby ball
B_{\ell/4}(z)
not intersecting
\Gamma_{0,t_{k-1}}
such that

{\bm{m}}_{0}(k-1)\left(\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}}\right)^{6-2\delta_{1}}\leq C\int_{\bar{\bm{\Phi}}_{k-1}(B_{\ell/4}(z))}|\bar{N}_{k-1}|^{2}\,.

The argument of
[
13
, Section 9]
provides now a similar bound for the ball
B^{\prime}=2\frac{t_{k-1}}{t_{k}}B_{\ell/4}(z)
, which has radius comparable to
1
, in the center manifold
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
. More precisely, since

\left(\frac{t_{k-1}}{t_{k}}\right)^{4}
is exactly the scaling relating the
L^{2}
norm on
B^{\prime}
and
B_{\ell/4}(z)
, while
\left(\frac{t_{k-1}}{t_{k}}\right)^{2-2\delta_{1}}
is the scaling factor which makes
{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)
and
{\bm{m}}_{0}(k-1)
comparable, the corresponding estimate is given by

{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)\leq C\int_{\bar{\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(B^{\prime})}|\bar{N}_{k}|^{2}\,.

Applying the rescaling which relates
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
, we find a corresponding rescaled ball
B^{\prime\prime}
(of radius comparable to
t_{k}
)

{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)t_{k}^{4}\leq C\int_{B^{\prime\prime}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}|N_{k}|^{2}\,.

Using that the center
z
of the ball can be chosen arbitrarily as long as it is at a distance from
L
compared to its diameter, we can ensure that
-d(p)^{-1}\phi^{\prime}(t_{k}^{-1}d(p))\geq ct_{k}^{-1}
on
B^{\prime\prime}
(for some positive geometric constant
c
). We thus get

{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)t_{k}^{3}\leq-\int_{B^{\prime\prime}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}|N_{k}|^{2}\frac{\phi^{\prime}(t_{k}^{-1}d(p))}{d(p)}\leq CH(N_{k},t_{k})\,.

However
{\mathbf{E}}_{k}(T,6t_{k})\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)
, and we have thus completed the proof of the second inequality in (
17.13
).

∎

Proof of Proposition
17.2
.

Define for
p\in{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
the map
F_{k}(p)=\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{p+(N_{k})_{i}(p)}\right\rrbracket
and for
q\in{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}
the map
F_{k-1}(q)=\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{q+(N_{k-1})_{i}(q)}\right\rrbracket
. Moreover denote by
{\mathbf{E}}_{k}:={\mathbf{E}}(T,6t_{k})
and
{\mathbf{C}}_{k}:={\mathbf{C}}_{2t_{k}}(0,V_{0})
. In order to compare
N_{k}
and
N_{k-1}
, we first apply Theorem
10.21
to the rescaled currents
T_{0,t_{k}}
and
T_{0,t_{k-1}}
to derive corresponding estimates for the normal approximations
\bar{N}_{k}
and
\bar{N}_{k-1}
of the currents on
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
and
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}
. We then scale them back to find corresponding estimates for
N_{k}
and
N_{k-1}
. During this process we also observe that, by (
17.9
) and (
17.10
), we have

{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)+{\bm{m}}_{0}(k-1)\left(\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}}\right)^{2-2\delta_{1}}\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}\,.

(17.14)

Moreover, we will prove later

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}\|_{C^{0}(B_{2t_{k}})}

\displaystyle\leq Ct_{k}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},

(17.15)

\displaystyle\|D{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}\|_{C^{0}(B_{2t_{k}})}

\displaystyle\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,

(17.16)

\displaystyle\|D^{2}{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}\|_{C^{0}(B_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}t_{k}})}

\displaystyle\leq Ct_{k-1}^{-1}{\bm{m}}_{0}(k-1)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\leq Ct_{k}^{-1}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,

(17.17)

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}\|_{C^{0}(B_{2t_{k}})}

\displaystyle\leq Ct_{k}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},

(17.18)

\displaystyle\|D{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}\|_{C^{0}(B_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}5}t_{k}})}

\displaystyle\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},

(17.19)

\displaystyle\|D^{2}{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}\|_{C^{0}(B_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}5}t_{k}})}

\displaystyle\leq Ct_{k}^{-1}{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\leq Ct_{k}^{-1}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,,

(17.20)

\displaystyle\|D({\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{2t_{k}})}

\displaystyle\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}^{1+2\gamma_{2}}\,.

(17.21)

In particular we get by (
17.14
), (
10.19
), and (
10.21
) after rescaling back

\displaystyle{\rm{Lip}}(N_{k})+{\rm{Lip}}(N_{k-1})

\displaystyle\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\gamma_{2}}\,,

(17.22)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{T}_{F_{k}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{k}-\mathbf{T}_{F_{k-1}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{k})

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{T}_{F_{k}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{k}-T\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{k})+{\mathbf{M}}(T\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{k}-\mathbf{T}_{F_{k-1}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{k})

\displaystyle\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+\gamma_{2}}\,.

(17.23)

Thus, we set
\hat{N}_{k}
to be the
Q
-valued function defined on
{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}
satisfying

\mathbf{G}_{\hat{N}_{k}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{k}=\mathbf{T}_{F_{k}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{k}=\mathbf{G}_{N_{k}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{k}=:S\,,

where with
\mathbf{G}_{\hat{N}_{k}}
we mean the current associated to the function
p\mapsto p+\hat{N}_{k}(p)
.
By comparing
D(N_{k},t_{k})
with
D(\hat{N}_{k},t_{k})
and
H(N_{k},t_{k})
with
H(\hat{N}_{k},t_{k})
we make an additional error of size
t_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+\gamma_{2}}
and size
t_{k}^{3}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+\gamma_{2}}
respectively. We will prove this later. With this aim in mind we change coordinates in the integrals of
D
and
H
to flat ones.
Denote by
{\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x):=(x,{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}(x))
and
{\bm{\Phi}}_{k-1}(x):=(x,{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}(x))
. We then estimate

\displaystyle\Big|D(N_{k},t_{k})-

\displaystyle\int|DN_{k}|^{2}({\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x))\phi\big(t_{k}^{-1}d({\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x))\big)dx\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{B_{2t_{k}}}|DN_{k}|^{2}({\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x))\phi\big(t_{k}^{-1}d({\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x))\big)\left|D{\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x)-({\rm Id}\,,0)\right|dx

\displaystyle\leq C\|D{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}\|_{C^{0}(B_{2t_{k}})}\int|DN_{k}|^{2}({\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x))\phi\big(t_{k}^{-1}d({\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x))\big)J{\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x)dx

\displaystyle\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\,,

where we used (
17.2
) and (
17.19
) for the last inequality.
Analogous estimates can be employed for
D(\hat{N}_{k},t_{k})
,
H(N_{k},t_{k})
, and
H(\hat{N}_{k},t_{k})
.

Therefore, it is enough to prove

\displaystyle\left|\int|DN_{k}|^{2}\phi\big(t_{k}^{-1}d({\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x))\big)dx-\int|D\hat{N}_{k}|^{2}\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\bm{\Phi}}_{k-1}(x)))dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+\gamma_{2}}\,,

(17.24)

\displaystyle\left|\int|N_{k}|^{2}\frac{\phi^{\prime}(t_{k}^{-1}d({\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x)))}{d({\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x))}dx-\int|\hat{N}_{k}|^{2}\frac{\phi^{\prime}(t_{k}^{-1}d({\bm{\Phi}}_{k-1}(x)))}{d({\bm{\Phi}}_{k-1}(x))}dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct_{k}^{3}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+\gamma_{2}}\,.

(17.25)

For (
17.24
), notice that
N_{k}(p)=\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{(F_{k})_{i}(p)-p}\right\rrbracket
. Hence, each component of
N_{k}
satisfies

|D(N_{k})_{i}({\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x))|\leq C\ |T_{(F_{k})_{i}(x)}\mathbf{T}_{F_{k}}-T_{{\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}|\,.

By the Lipschitz bound of
{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}
(
17.19
) and of
F_{k}
, we thus have

\displaystyle\int|DN_{k}|^{2}\phi\big(t_{k}^{-1}d({\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x))\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbf{C}}|\vec{S}(p)-\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}|^{2}\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)))d\|S\|(p)+O(t_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+\gamma_{2}})\,,

\displaystyle\int|D\hat{N}_{k}|^{2}\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\bm{\Phi}}_{k-1}(x)))

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbf{C}}|\vec{S}(p)-\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k-1}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}|^{2}\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\mathbf{p}}_{k-1}(p)))d\|S\|(p)

\displaystyle\quad+O(t_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+\gamma_{2}})\,,

where we denoted by
{\mathbf{p}}_{k}
and
{\mathbf{p}}_{k-1}
the nearest point projection on
{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}
respectively, while
{\mathbf{C}}
is the vertical cylinder with base
B_{2t_{k}}
. As we have from Theorem
10.16
that
\|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}\|_{C^{2}}\leq Ct_{k}^{-1}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
, by the Lipschitz bound of
\phi
, we deduce for any
p\in{\rm spt}(S)
and
q,q^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
,

\displaystyle|\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)))-\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\mathbf{p}}_{k-1}(p)))|

\displaystyle\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1/2}\,,

\displaystyle|T_{q}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}-T_{q^{\prime}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq Ct_{k}^{-1}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}|q-q^{\prime}|\,.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{C}}|\vec{S}(p)-\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}|^{2}|\phi(t_{k}^{-1}

\displaystyle d({\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)))-\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\mathbf{p}}_{k-1}(p))|d\|S\|(p)\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\,,

\displaystyle|T_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}-T_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k-1}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}|

\displaystyle\leq C|D{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}({\mathbf{p}}_{V_{0}}({\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)))-D{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}({\mathbf{p}}_{V_{0}}({\mathbf{p}}_{k-1}(p)))|

\displaystyle\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}+|D({\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1})|({\mathbf{p}}_{V_{0}}(p))

where we used (
10.19
) in the last inequality. We therefore can conclude

\displaystyle\left|\int|DN_{k}|^{2}\phi\big(t_{k}^{-1}d({\bm{\Phi}}_{k}(x))\big)dx-\int_{B_{2t_{k}}}|D\hat{N}_{k}|^{2}\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\bm{\Phi}}_{k-1}(x)))dx\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+\gamma_{2}}+C\int_{\mathbf{C}}|\vec{S}(p)-\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}|^{2}|\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)))-\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\mathbf{p}}_{k-1}(p)))|\ d\|S\|

\displaystyle\qquad+C\int_{\mathbf{C}}\left||\vec{S}(p)-\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}|^{2}-|\vec{S}(p)-\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k-1}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}|^{2}\right|\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\mathbf{p}}_{k{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}-1}}(p)))\ d\|S\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+\gamma_{2}}+C\int_{\mathbf{C}}|\vec{S}(p)-\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}||\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}-\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k-1}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}|\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)))\ d\|S\|

\displaystyle\qquad+C\int_{\mathbf{C}}|\vec{S}(p)-\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k-1}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}||\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}-\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k-1}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}|\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)))\ d\|S\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+\gamma_{2}}+Ct_{k}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\left(\int_{\mathbf{C}}|\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}-\vec{T}_{{\mathbf{p}}_{k-1}(p)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}|^{2}\phi(t_{k}^{-1}d({\mathbf{p}}_{k}(p)))\ d\|S\|\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+\gamma_{2}}+Ct_{k}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1/2}\left(\int_{B_{2t_{k}}}|D{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-D{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+\gamma_{2}}\,,

where we used (
17.21
) for the last inequality.

We finally turn to (
17.25
). For
x\in V_{0}
, denote by
z_{k}:=(x,{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}(x))
and
\hat{z}_{k}:=(x,{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}(x))
. Then we estimate

\displaystyle\left||N_{k}|^{2}(z_{k})-|\hat{N}_{k}|^{2}(\hat{z}_{k})\right|\leq|N_{k}|(z_{k})\left||N_{k}|(z_{k})-|\hat{N}_{k}|(\hat{z}_{k})\right|+|\hat{N}_{k}|(\hat{z})\left||N_{k}|(z_{k})-|\hat{N}_{k}|(\hat{z}_{k})\right|\,.

Moreover, using Cauchy-Schwarz and the fact that the
L^{2}
norm of
N_{k}
and
\hat{N}_{k}
is bounded by
t_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1/2}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int|N_{k}|^{2}\frac{\phi^{\prime}(t_{k}^{-1}d(z_{k}))}{d(z_{k})}dx-\int|\hat{N}_{k}|^{2}\frac{\phi^{\prime}(d((\hat{z}_{k}))}{d(\hat{z}_{k})}dx\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq Ct_{k}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\left(\int_{B_{2t_{k}}}\left||N_{k}|(z_{k})-|\hat{N}_{k}|(\hat{z}_{k})\right|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(17.26)

If we now define
p_{i}:=(F_{k})_{i}(x)
and
q_{i}:=(\hat{F}_{k})_{i}(x):=\hat{z}_{k}+(\hat{N}_{k})_{i}(\hat{z}_{k})
, we have (up to reordering the indices)

\displaystyle|N_{k}|(z_{k})=\left(\sum_{i}|p_{i}-z_{k}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\qquad|\hat{N}_{k}|(\hat{z}_{k})=\left(\sum_{i}|q_{i}-\hat{z}_{k}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Now we use the triangle inequality to see

\displaystyle\left||N_{k}|(z_{k})-|\hat{N}_{k}|(\hat{z}_{k})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\Big(\sum_{i}|p_{i}-z_{k}|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}-\Big(\sum_{i}|q_{i}-\hat{z}_{k}|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{i}|p_{i}-q_{\sigma(i)}|^{2}+C|z_{k}-\hat{z}_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle=C{\mathcal{G}}\Big(\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{p_{i}}\right\rrbracket,\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{q_{i}}\right\rrbracket\Big)^{2}+C|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}(x)-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}(x)|^{2}\,\,,

for
\sigma
the permutation realizing the distance
{\mathcal{G}}\Big(\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{p_{i}}\right\rrbracket,\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{q_{i}}\right\rrbracket\Big)
.

Note that, since
{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}
and
{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}
agree on the boundary
{\mathbf{p}}_{V_{0}}(\Gamma)
, we can use (
17.21
) and the Poincaré inequality to conclude

\|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}\|_{L^{2}(B_{2t_{k}})}\leq Ct_{k}\|D{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-D{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}\|_{L^{2}(B_{2t_{k}})}\leq Ct_{k}^{3}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1/2+\gamma_{2}}\,.

(17.27)

Figure 4.
An illustration of how Lemma
17.3
is used.

To estimate further we split the distance
{\mathcal{G}}\Big(\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{p_{i}}\right\rrbracket,\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{q_{i}}\right\rrbracket\Big)
into a horizontal and vertical part in the following sense. We define
V:=\hat{z}_{k}+T_{\hat{z}_{k}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}
,
\tilde{V}:=z_{k}+T_{z_{k}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
,
V^{\prime}:=\hat{z}_{k}+T_{z_{k}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
and
\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{q_{i}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket:=\langle S,{\mathbf{p}}_{V^{\prime}},0\rangle
. Observe that
V
and
V^{\prime}
differ by a rotation, while
V^{\prime}
and
\tilde{V}
are parallel. We then apply the Lemma
17.3
to the shifted situation where
\hat{z}_{k}=0
and deduce

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}\Big(\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{q_{i}}\right\rrbracket,\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{q_{i}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C{\rm{Lip}}(F_{k})\|N_{k}\|_{C^{0}}(|V-V_{0}|+|V^{\prime}-V_{0}|)

\displaystyle\leq C{\rm{Lip}}(F_{k})\|N_{k}\|_{C^{0}}(|D{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}|+|D{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}|)

\displaystyle\leq Ct_{k}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}+\gamma_{2}}\,,

where in the last inequality we used (
17.16
) and (
17.19
).
In order to estimate
{\mathcal{G}}\Big(\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{p_{i}}\right\rrbracket,\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{q_{i}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket\Big)
,
we call
f_{\tilde{V}}:T_{z_{k}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
the function having the same graph as
F_{k}
in
{\mathbf{C}}_{2t_{k}}
. Observe that

\left|T_{z_{k}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}-V_{0}\right|\leq Ct_{k}\|D^{2}{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}\|_{C^{0}}\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}

and by
[
12
, Proposition 5.2]

{\rm{Lip}}(f_{\tilde{V}})\leq C{\mathbf{E}}^{\gamma_{2}}_{k}\,.

Then we observe that
\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{p_{i}}\right\rrbracket=\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{{f_{\tilde{V}}}_{i}(z_{k})}\right\rrbracket
and
\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{q^{\prime}_{i}}\right\rrbracket=\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{{f_{\tilde{V}}}_{i}({\mathbf{p}}_{T_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}(\hat{z}_{k}))}\right\rrbracket
. Thus we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}\Big(\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{p_{i}}\right\rrbracket,\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{q_{i}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket\Big)

\displaystyle\leq{\rm{Lip}}(f_{\tilde{V}})|z_{k}-{\mathbf{p}}_{T_{z_{k}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}}(\hat{z}_{k})|

\displaystyle\leq{\rm{Lip}}(f_{\tilde{V}})(||{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}||_{C^{0}}+||{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}||_{C^{0}})\leq Ct_{k}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}+\gamma_{2}}.

Squaring and integrating (and using (
17.27
)), we deduce

\int_{B_{2t_{k}}}\left||N_{k}|(z_{k})-|\hat{N}_{k}|(\hat{z}_{k})\right|^{2}\leq Ct_{k}^{4}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+2\gamma_{2}}\,.

Inserting in (
17
) we conclude

\left|\int|N_{k}|^{2}\frac{\phi^{\prime}(t_{k}^{-1}d(z_{k})}{d(z_{k})}dx-\int|\hat{N}_{k}|^{2}\frac{\phi^{\prime}(t_{k}^{-1}d((\hat{z}))}{d(\hat{z})}dx\right|\leq Ct_{k}^{3}{\mathbf{E}}^{1+\gamma_{2}}\,.

It remains to prove (
17.15
)-(
17.21
).

(
17.19
) and (
17.20
) follow from Theorem
10.16
using a simple rescaling and (
17.14
).
Next, for
{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}
the estimate on the second derivative derived from Theorem
10.16
and (
17.14
) is favourable, as it gives directly (
17.17
).
However the estimate on the first derivative is not, as it would give

\|D{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}\|_{C^{0}(B_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}5}t_{k}})}\leq C{\bm{m}}_{0}(k-1)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\leq C\left(\frac{t_{k-1}}{t_{k}}\right)^{1-\delta_{1}}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,,

(17.28)

which is not good enough for our purposes.

Proof of (
17.15
), (
17.16
), and (
17.18
)

In order to gain a more favorable estimate for the first derivative (and the
C^{0}
norm of
{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1})
we first observe that by Lemma
10.4

\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{10t_{k}}(0,V_{0}))\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}t_{k}\,.

Arguing as in the proof of (
17.3
) it is not difficult to see that

\int_{{\mathbf{C}}_{5t_{k}}(0,V_{0})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}}|N_{k-1}|^{2}\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}t_{k}^{4}\,.

(17.29)

Since
\mathbf{T}_{F_{k-1}}
coincides with
{\rm spt}(T)
on a large set we can also infer

\int_{B_{5t_{k}}}|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}|^{2}\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}t_{k}^{4}\,.

(17.30)

In order to see the latter estimate, consider first a point
p\in{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}
with the property that the support of
F_{k-1}(p)
is a subset of the support of
T
. By the height bound we know that
\mathbf{h}(T,{\mathbf{C}}_{10t_{k}}(0,V_{0}))\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1/2}t_{k}
. In particular, if we let
{\mathbf{p}}_{0}^{\perp}
be the projection on the orthogonal complement
V_{0}
, we conclude

|{\mathbf{p}}_{0}^{\perp}\circ F_{k-1}|(p)\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1/2}t_{k}\,.

Consider now that, if
x
is such that
p=(x,{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}(x))
, since
F_{k}(p)=\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{F_{k}^{i}(p)}\right\rrbracket=\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{N_{k}^{i}(p)+p}\right\rrbracket
, we get

\displaystyle|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|{\mathbf{p}}_{0}^{\perp}\circ F_{k-1}|(x,{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}(x))+|{\mathbf{p}}_{0}^{\perp}\circ N_{k-1}|(x,{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}(x))

\displaystyle\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1/2}t_{k}+|N_{k-1}|(x,{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}(x))\,.

(17.31)

Let now
\mathcal{K}
be the set of such points
p
(i.e. for which the support of
F_{k}(p)
is contained in the support of
T
) and define
K:={\mathbf{p}}_{0}(\mathcal{K})\cap B_{5t_{k}}
. Using the bounds (
17.29
) and (
17.31
) we easily obtain

\int_{K}|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}(x)|^{2}\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}t_{k}^{4}\,.

(17.32)

In order to estimate the integral on the remaining portion (i.e. on
B_{5t_{k}}\setminus K
), we apply (
10.16
) to
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}
, sum over all the stopped squares in
B_{5t_{k}}\setminus K
(which by the stopping condition have side length comparable to
t_{k}/t_{k-1}
), scale it back to
{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}
and deduce

\displaystyle|B_{5t_{k}}\setminus K|

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbf{B}}_{6t_{k}}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}\setminus\mathcal{K})\leq C({\bm{m}}_{0}(k-1))^{1+\gamma_{2}}\left(\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}}\right)^{4+\gamma_{2}}t_{k-1}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}}\right)^{2+\gamma_{2}}\,.

(17.33)

Then we observe that, by (
17.32
) and the classical Chebyshev inequality, there is at least one point
x\in B_{5t_{k}}
where
|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}(x)|\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1/2}t_{k}
, and we use (
17.28
) to conclude that for all
y\in B_{5t_{k}}
we have

|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}(y)|\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}t_{k}+C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\left(\frac{t_{k-1}}{t_{k}}\right)^{1-\delta_{1}}|x-y|\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\left(\frac{t_{k-1}}{t_{k}}\right)^{1-\delta_{1}}t_{k}\,.

(17.34)

Putting together (
17.32
), (
17.33
), and (
17.34
), we achieve

\int_{B_{5t_{k}}}|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}|^{2}\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}t_{k}^{4}+C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}\left(\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}}\right)^{2+\gamma_{2}-2(1-\delta_{1})}t_{k}^{4}\,.

Since
2+\gamma_{2}\geq 2-2\delta_{1}
and
t_{k}\leq t_{k-1}
, the latter clearly implies (
17.30
).

We next use Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality and from (
17.29
) and (
17.17
) we get (
17.15
) and (
17.16
), namely

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}\|_{C^{0}(B_{2t_{k}})}\leq Ct_{k}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},\qquad\|D{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}\|_{C^{0}(B_{2t_{k}})}\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,.

We analogously conclude (
17.18
).

Proof of (
17.21
)

We wish to show that

\|D({\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{2t_{k}})}\leq Ct_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+2\gamma_{2}}\,.

We choose a suitable cut-off function
\psi
which equals
1
on
B_{2t_{k}}
and is compactly supported in
B_{3t_{k}}
and write

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2t_{k}}}|D({\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1})|^{2}\leq\int_{B_{3t_{k}}}|D({\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1})|^{2}\psi

Integrating by parts, we can estimate

\displaystyle\int|D({\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1})|^{2}\psi=\int({\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1})\Delta({\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1})\psi+\int({\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1})\nabla({\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1})\cdot\nabla\psi\,.

We next use that
\|\nabla\psi\|\leq Ct_{k}^{-1}
, (
17.16
), (
17.17
), (
17.19
), and (
17.20
) to estimate

\int_{B_{2t_{k}}}|D({\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1})|^{2}\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1/2}t_{k}^{-1}\int_{B_{3t_{k}}}|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}|\,.

(17.35)

We next consider the multivalued functions
f_{k}
and
f_{k-1}
on
B_{3t_{k}}
and taking values into
\mathcal{A}_{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the properties that

\mathbf{G}_{f_{k}}=\mathbf{T}_{F_{k}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{0,3t_{k}}\,,\qquad\mathbf{G}_{f_{k-1}}=\mathbf{T}_{F_{k-1}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.3pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.3pt,width=5.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\mathbf{C}}_{0,3t_{k}}\,.

Note that the values of
f_{k}
and
f_{k-1}
coincide except for a set of measure at most
t_{k}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+\gamma_{2}}
(again we use Theorem
10.21
and sum over the stopped squares). Moreover, because
{\rm{Lip}}(f_{k}),{\rm{Lip}}(f_{k-1})\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\gamma_{2}}
, we immediately draw the conclusion

\int_{B_{3t_{k}}}|{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{k}-{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{k-1}|\leq{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1+2\gamma_{2}}t_{k}^{3}\,.

On the other hand, appealing to Proposition
10.23
(and rescaling appropriately) we get

\displaystyle\int_{B_{3t_{k}}}|{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{3/4}t_{k}^{3}\,,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{3t_{k}}}|{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{k-1}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left(\frac{t_{k-1}}{t_{k}}\right)^{2-2\delta_{1}}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}\right)^{3/4}\left(\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}}\right)^{4}t_{k-1}^{3}\,.

While the first estimate is already suitable for our purposes, the second require some more care. We recall (
17.10
)
to the effect that

\left(\frac{t_{k-1}}{t_{k}}\right)^{2-2\delta_{1}}{\mathbf{E}}_{k}\leq\left(\frac{t_{k-1}}{t_{k}}\right)^{2-2\delta_{1}}{\bm{m}}_{0}(k)\leq C

for a geometric constant
C
. Since
\frac{1}{2-2\delta_{1}}\geq\frac{3}{4}
, we can then estimate

\int_{B_{3t_{k}}}|{\bm{\eta}}\circ f_{k-1}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}|\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{\frac{3}{4}}t_{k}^{3}\,.

By possible choosing
\gamma_{2}
sufficiently small we get

\int_{B_{3t_{k}}}|{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}-{\bm{\varphi}}_{k-1}|\leq C{\mathbf{E}}_{k}^{1/2+2\gamma_{2}}t_{k}^{3}\,,

which, by (
17.35
), gives (
17.21
).

∎

17.1.
Lipschitz estimate using 2d-rotations

Lemma 17.3
.

There is a constant
c>0
such that the following holds. Let
F:V_{0}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be a Lipschitz map with
{\rm{Lip}}(F)<c
, let
V
and
V^{\prime}
be
2
-dimensional subspaces with
|V-V_{0}|+|V^{\prime}-V_{0}|<c
and denote by
{\mathbf{p}}
and
{\mathbf{p}}^{\prime}
the orthogonal projection on
V
and
V^{\prime}
respectively. Then for
P:=\langle\mathbf{T}_{F},{\mathbf{p}},0\rangle
and
P^{\prime}:=\langle\mathbf{T}_{F},{\mathbf{p}}^{\prime},0\rangle
it holds

{\mathcal{G}}(P,P^{\prime})\leq C\,{\rm{Lip}}(F)\,\|F\|_{C^{0}}(|V-V_{0}|+|V^{\prime}-V_{0}|)\,.

(17.36)

Proof.

We use an argument already observed in more generality in
[
13
, Lemma D.1]
. However, we repeat here the parts needed for the previous lemma. First of all, we construct finitely many planes by using 2d-rotations that will allow us to reduce (
17.36
) to a one-dimensional situation. Recall the terminology: we say that
R\in\text{SO}(n+2)
is a 2d-rotation if there are two orthonormal vectors
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
and an angle
\theta
such that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}R(e_{1})&=\cos(\theta)e_{1}+\sin(\theta)e_{2}\,,\\
R(e_{2})&=\cos(\theta)e_{1}-\sin(\theta)e_{2}\,,\\
R(v)&=v\,,\qquad\text{ for any }v\in\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle^{\perp}.\end{cases}

Now let us denote by
W_{1}=V\cap V^{\prime}
. If
\dim(W_{1})=2
, then
V=V^{\prime}
and there is nothing to prove. Otherwise
\dim(W_{1})<2=\dim(V)=\dim(V^{\prime})
and we can write

V=W_{1}\oplus\hat{V},\qquad V^{\prime}=W_{1}\oplus\hat{V}^{\prime},

for some subspaces
\hat{V}
and
\hat{V}^{\prime}
.
Choose any unit vector
e_{1}\in\hat{V}=V\cap W_{1}^{\perp}
and define

e^{\prime}_{1}:=\frac{{\mathbf{p}}^{\prime}(e_{1})}{|{\mathbf{p}}^{\prime}(e_{1})|}\in V^{\prime}\cap W_{1}^{\perp}\,.

Moreover, define
R_{1}
to be the 2d-rotation mapping
e_{1}
onto
e^{\prime}_{1}
and

\displaystyle V_{2}

\displaystyle:=R_{1}(V)\,,

\displaystyle W_{2}

\displaystyle:=V_{2}\cap V^{\prime}\,.

Notice that
W_{1}\subset V_{1}
is invariant under
R_{1}
, so clearly
W_{1}=(W_{1}\cap V^{\prime})\subset(V_{2}\cap V^{\prime})=W_{2}
. Moreover,
e^{\prime}_{1}\in V_{2}\cap V^{\prime}
, and hence

W_{2}\supset\langle W_{1},e^{\prime}_{1}\rangle\,.

As
e^{\prime}_{1}\perp W_{1}
, we have
\dim(W_{2})\geq\dim(W_{1})+1
.
Now, if
\dim(W_{2})=2
, then
V_{2}=R_{1}(V_{1})=V^{\prime}
and we define
R_{2}
to be the identity. Otherwise
\dim(W_{2})=1
and we can again find a unit vector
e_{2}\in V_{2}\cap W_{2}^{\perp}
, define

e^{\prime}_{2}:=\frac{{\mathbf{p}}^{\prime}(e_{2})}{|{\mathbf{p}}^{\prime}(e_{2})|}\in V^{\prime}\cap W_{1}^{\perp}\,,

and define
R_{2}
to be the 2d-rotation mapping
e_{2}
onto
e^{\prime}_{2}
. As before, we denote by
V_{3}:=R_{2}(V_{2})
and observe that
W_{3}:=V_{3}\cap V^{\prime}
has at least one dimension more than
W_{2}
. Thus, in both cases we have

V^{\prime}=R_{2}\circ R_{1}(V)\,.

Next, denote by
V_{1}:=V
and for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
the orthogonal projection onto
V_{j}
by
{\mathbf{p}}_{j}
and
P_{j}:=\langle\mathbf{T}_{F},{\mathbf{p}}_{j},0\rangle
. Notice that for
c>0
small enough,
{\rm spt}(P_{j})
is a
Q
-valued point. We claim

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}(P_{j},P_{j+1})\leq C{\rm{Lip}}(F)\,\|F\|_{C^{0}}(|V_{j}-V_{0}|+|V_{j+1}-V_{0}|)

concluding the lemma as
|V_{j}-V_{0}|\leq|V-V^{\prime}|+|V-V_{0}|\leq 2(|V-V_{0}|+|V^{\prime}-V_{0}|)
for every
j
.
Indeed, for each
j
, fix a unit vector
v_{j}\in V_{0}
such that

\langle e_{j},e^{\prime}_{j}\rangle\cap V_{0}=\{t\cdot v_{j}:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\}\,.

Then we can apply the selection principle
[
10
, Proposition 1.2]
to the map
F^{j}(t):=F(tv_{j})
to get a selection

F^{j}=\sum_{i}\left\llbracket{F_{i}^{j}}\right\rrbracket

for some Lipschitz functions
F_{i}^{j}:[-1,1]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
satisfying

|DF_{i}^{j}|\leq|DF|\leq{\rm{Lip}}(F)\qquad\text{a.e.}

(17.37)

We therefore conclude the existence of points
s_{1}^{j},\dots,s_{Q}^{j},s_{1}^{j+1},\dots,s_{Q}^{j+1}\in[-1,1]
such that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}(P_{j},P_{j+1})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i}\left|F^{j}_{i}(s_{i}^{j})-F^{j}_{i}(s_{i}^{j+1})\right|

\displaystyle\leq{\rm{Lip}}(F)\sum_{i}\left|s_{i}^{j}-s_{i}^{j+1}\right|

\displaystyle\leq{\rm{Lip}}(F)\sum_{i}\left(|s_{i}^{j}|+|s_{i}^{j+1}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq QC\,{\rm{Lip}}(F)\,\|F\|_{C^{0}}\left(|V_{j}-V_{0}|+|V_{j+1}-V_{0}|\right)\,,

where we also have used (
17.37
).
∎

18.
